Start here — in plain words

Algebraic topology studies shapes by attaching algebraic objects that stay the same under continuous deformations.

  1. Imagine a coffee mug and a donut: they can be stretched into each other without tearing, so they share the same “hole count.”
  2. It lets mathematicians tell spaces apart and solve geometric problems by doing algebra instead.
  3. Remember: spaces with different algebraic invariants cannot be continuously deformed into each other.
🔬 ScientistPure mathematics — no exam syllabus

Algebraic Topology — Attaching Algebraic Invariants to Spaces

Algebraic topology uses algebra to study topological spaces. It assigns algebraic objects — groups, rings, modules — to spaces in a way that's invariant under homeomorphism. This transforms geometric questions into algebraic ones. Two spaces are different if their algebraic invariants differ. The field began with Poincaré's 1895 Analysis Situs and has since grown into a vast web of connections between topology, algebra, and geometry.

"Algebraic topology is the art of turning topology into algebra and then solving the algebra."
— Dennis Sullivan
Part 1

🔄 Homotopy and the Fundamental Group

Loops and Deformations
The Fundamental Group — Detecting Holes via Loops
Two continuous maps f,g: X → Y are homotopic (f ≃ g) if one can be continuously deformed into the other. A homotopy is a map H: X×[0,1] → Y with H(x,0)=f(x), H(x,1)=g(x). The fundamental group π₁(X,x₀) has elements that are homotopy classes of loops (paths starting and ending at x₀). The group operation is concatenation of loops. For the circle S¹, π₁(S¹) ≅ ℤ — loops are classified by winding number. For S², π₁(S²) = 0 — every loop shrinks to a point (the sphere is simply connected). The fundamental group of the figure-8 is the free group on two generators F₂. The Seifert-van Kampen theorem computes π₁ of a union: if X = U ∪ V with U∩V path-connected, then π₁(X) is the amalgamated free product of π₁(U) and π₁(V) over π₁(U∩V). This is the main computational tool for fundamental groups.
The fundamental group captures 1-dimensional holes — loops that can't be shrunk to a point.
Part 2

🏗️ Covering Spaces — Unwinding the Structure

The Universal Cover
Covering Spaces — When a Space Unrolls Into Its Universal Version
A covering space of X is a space X̃ with a map p: X̃ → X such that every point x ∈ X has a neighborhood U evenly covered by p (p^{-1}(U) is a disjoint union of copies of U). The circle S¹ is covered by ℝ via p(t) = e^{2πit}. The universal cover of the torus is ℝ². Covering spaces are intimately tied to the fundamental group: for reasonable spaces, connected coverings correspond to subgroups of π₁. The universal cover X̃ is the simply connected covering (π₁(X̃) = 0). The deck transformation group of the universal cover is π₁(X). The lifting criterion: a map f: Y → X lifts to X̃ iff f_*(π₁(Y)) ⊆ p_*(π₁(X̃)). This connects covering space theory to Galois theory — the analogy is precise and leads to the étale fundamental group in algebraic geometry.
Covering spaces unwind the holes of a space into a simply connected version — the fundamental group acts as symmetries of the unrolling.
Part 3

🧮 Homology — Counting Holes Algebraically

The Groups Hₙ
Homology — Holes of Any Dimension
While the fundamental group detects 1-dimensional holes, homology groups Hₙ(X) detect holes in every dimension. A singular n-simplex is a continuous map σ: Δⁿ → X from the standard n-simplex. The boundary map ∂ₙ: Cₙ → C_{n−1} sends an n-simplex to the alternating sum of its (n−1)-dimensional faces. The singular chain complex is ... → C₂ → C₁ → C₀ → 0 with ∂_{n−1}∘∂ₙ = 0. Define cycles Zₙ = ker ∂ₙ, boundaries Bₙ = im ∂_{n+1}. The nth homology group Hₙ(X) = Zₙ / Bₙ captures n-dimensional cycles modulo boundaries. H₀ counts path components. H₁ is the abelianization of π₁. For a surface of genus g, H₁ ≅ ℤ^{2g}. The Euler characteristic χ = Σ (−1)ⁿ rank(Hₙ). Cellular homology computes Hₙ from a CW structure — it's much more computable than singular homology. For a CW complex with cₙ n-cells, χ = Σ (−1)ⁿ cₙ.
Homology groups detect holes in every dimension — they're abelian, computable, and functorial.
Part 4

🌌 Cohomology and Poincaré Duality

The Dual Perspective
Cohomology — Cup Products and the Structure of Space
Cohomology Hⁿ(X) is the dual of homology: Hⁿ(X) = Hom(Hₙ(X), ℤ) (with more nuance). But cohomology has extra structure that homology lacks: the cup product ⌣: Hᵖ × H^q → H^{p+q}, making the cohomology of a space into a graded ring. The cup product captures how holes interact — it's the algebraic version of intersection of cycles. Poincaré duality says for a closed oriented n-manifold M, Hᵏ(M) ≅ H_{n−k}(M). For a surface of genus g, H₀ ≅ ℤ, H₁ ≅ ℤ^{2g}, H₂ ≅ ℤ, and Poincaré duality pairs H¹ with H₁. The Künneth formula computes Hₙ(X×Y) from Hₚ(X) and H_q(Y). Leray-Serre spectral sequences compute cohomology of fiber bundles. Modern algebraic topology extends these ideas to extraordinary cohomology theories like K-theory (vector bundles) and cobordism. The Atiyah-Singer index theorem connects the analysis of elliptic operators on a manifold to its topology — one of the deepest results of 20th-century mathematics.
Cohomology rings and Poincaré duality give a complete algebraic picture of a manifold's topology — the cup product tells us how cycles intersect.

🎯 Practice — Check Your Algebraic Topology Intuition

These puzzles build algebraic topology intuition.

Higher Homotopy Groups and the Hurewicz Theorem

The fundamental group π₁(X, x₀) captures only 1-dimensional holes. For n ≥ 2, the higher homotopy groups πₙ(X, x₀) are defined as homotopy classes of maps (Sⁿ, s₀) → (X, x₀) where Sⁿ is the n-sphere with basepoint s₀. Unlike π₁, all higher homotopy groups are abelian — the Eckmann-Hilton argument shows that the two natural compositions (horizontal and vertical concatenation of n-cubes) coincide and are commutative. The groups πₙ(Sⁿ) ≅ ℤ for all n ≥ 1, generated by the identity map. However, πₙ(Sᵏ) for n > k are notoriously complex: π₃(S²) ≅ ℤ (the Hopf fibration), π₄(S³) ≅ ℤ/2, π₅(S³) ≅ ℤ/2, and the stable homotopy groups of spheres πₙ₊ₖ(Sⁿ) for large n form one of the central computational challenges of algebraic topology.

The Hurewicz theorem provides the fundamental bridge between homotopy and homology. For a path-connected space X, the Hurewicz homomorphism h: πₙ(X) → Hₙ(X) sends a homotopy class [f: Sⁿ → X] to the image of the fundamental class [Sⁿ] under f_*: Hₙ(Sⁿ) → Hₙ(X). The theorem states: if X is (n−1)-connected (i.e., πᵢ(X) = 0 for i < n), then h is an isomorphism for i = n and an epimorphism for i = n+1. In particular, for a simply connected space, π₂(X) ≅ H₂(X) and π₃(X) → H₃(X) is surjective. This allows computation of low-dimensional homotopy groups via homology, which is often more tractable. The relative Hurewicz theorem extends this to pairs (X, A) and is essential for the proof of the Whitehead theorem: a map between simply connected CW complexes inducing isomorphisms on all homotopy groups is a homotopy equivalence.

Postnikov towers decompose a space into its homotopy groups. A Postnikov tower for X is a sequence of spaces Xₙ with maps X → Xₙ inducing isomorphisms on πᵢ for i ≤ n and πᵢ(Xₙ) = 0 for i > n. Each stage Xₙ is obtained from Xₙ₋₁ by attaching cells of dimension ≥ n+2 to kill higher homotopy groups. The k-invariants (Postnikov invariants) are cohomology classes in Hⁿ⁺¹(Xₙ₋₁; πₙ(X)) that classify the fibration Xₙ → Xₙ₋₁. This reduces the classification of homotopy types to algebraic data: a sequence of groups πₙ and k-invariants. For simply connected spaces of finite type, this gives a complete algebraic model.

Fibrations, Cofibrations, and the Long Exact Sequence of Homotopy Groups

A fibration in the sense of Serre is a map p: E → B with the homotopy lifting property for all CW complexes: given a homotopy H: X × I → B and a lift of H₀: X → E, there exists a lift H̃: X × I → E extending the given lift. The fiber F = p⁻¹(b₀) over a basepoint b₀ ∈ B sits in a long exact sequence of homotopy groups: ... → πₙ₊₁(B) → πₙ(F) → πₙ(E) → πₙ(B) → πₙ₋₁(F) → ... This is the primary tool for computing homotopy groups of total spaces from fibers and bases, or vice versa. The connecting homomorphism πₙ₊₁(B) → πₙ(F) sends a map (Dⁿ⁺¹, ∂Dⁿ⁺¹) → (B, b₀) to the restriction of its lift to ∂Dⁿ⁺¹ ≅ Sⁿ, which lands in F.

Classic examples: the path-loop fibration ΩX → PX → X where PX is the space of paths starting at x₀ (contractible) and ΩX is the loop space. This gives πₙ(ΩX) ≅ πₙ₊₁(X). The Hopf fibrations S¹ → S³ → S², S³ → S⁷ → S⁴, S⁷ → S¹⁵ → S⁸ arise from division algebras (ℂ, ℍ, 𝕆) and yield π₃(S²) ≅ ℤ, π₇(S⁴) ≅ ℤ ⊕ ℤ/12, etc. The fibration SO(n) → SO(n+1) → Sⁿ gives πₙ(SO(n+1)) ≅ πₙ(SO(n)) ⊕ πₙ(Sⁿ) for n ≥ 3, leading to Bott periodicity: πₖ₊₈(O) ≅ πₖ(O) for the stable orthogonal group O = colim SO(n).

Cofibrations are the dual notion: a map i: A → X with the homotopy extension property. The mapping cone Cᵢ = X ∪ᵢ CA gives a cofibration sequence A → X → Cᵢ → ΣA → ΣX → ... where Σ is suspension. This induces a long exact sequence in homology and, for simply connected spaces, in homotopy. The Puppe sequence extends this to an infinite sequence of cofibrations. Fibrations and cofibrations form a model category structure on topological spaces (Quillen), where weak equivalences are weak homotopy equivalences. This abstract framework underlies modern homotopy theory and allows the construction of derived functors like Ext and Tor in topological contexts.

Worked Computation: π₃(S²) via the Hopf Fibration
Use the Hopf fibration S¹ → S³ → S² to compute π₃(S²).
The Hopf fibration is a fiber sequence S¹ → S³ → S². The long exact sequence in homotopy gives: ... → π₃(S¹) → π₃(S³) → π₃(S²) → π₂(S¹) → π₂(S³) → ... Since S¹ is a K(ℤ,1), πₙ(S¹) = 0 for n ≥ 2. Thus π₃(S¹) = 0 and π₂(S¹) = 0. Also π₂(S³) = 0 (S³ is 2-connected). The sequence reduces to 0 → π₃(S³) → π₃(S²) → 0. Since π₃(S³) ≅ ℤ (generated by the identity), the map is an isomorphism. Therefore π₃(S²) ≅ ℤ. The generator corresponds to the Hopf map η: S³ → S², which has Hopf invariant 1.
Exam Strategy: Long Exact Sequences
When a fibration F → E → B appears, immediately write the long exact sequence of homotopy groups. Identify which terms vanish (e.g., πₙ(S¹)=0 for n≥2, πₙ(Sᵏ)=0 for n<k). The connecting homomorphism often gives the only nontrivial map. For spheres, remember πₙ(Sⁿ)=ℤ and πₙ(Sᵏ)=0 for n<k. The Hopf fibrations S¹→S³→S², S³→S⁷→S⁴, S⁷→S¹⁵→S⁸ are the only sphere fibrations with sphere fibers and total spaces (Adams' theorem).</div>

Spectral Sequences — The Serre Spectral Sequence

Spectral sequences are the computational engine of modern algebraic topology. A spectral sequence is a sequence of differential graded modules (Eʳ, dʳ) with dʳ of bidegree (r, 1−r) such that Eʳ⁺¹ = H(Eʳ, dʳ). The Serre spectral sequence applies to a fibration F → E → B with B a path-connected CW complex and the action of π₁(B) on H*(F) trivial (e.g., B simply connected). It has E₂-page E₂p,q = Hᵖ(B; H^q(F)) converging to Hp+q(E). The differentials dʳ: Eʳp,q → Eʳp+r, q−r+1 measure the failure of cohomology classes on the base to lift to the total space, or the obstruction to extending classes from the fiber.

The filtration on E comes from the preimages of the skeleta of B: Fₚ = p⁻¹(B⁽ᵖ⁾). The associated graded pieces are H*(Fₚ, Fₚ₋₁) ≅ H*(B⁽ᵖ⁾, B⁽ᵖ⁻¹⁾; H*(F)) ≅ Cᵖ(B; H*(F)), giving the E₁-page. The E₂-page is the cohomology of this complex, i.e., H*(B; H*(F)). Convergence means there is a filtration on H*(E) whose associated graded is E_∞. The edge homomorphisms give the restriction H*(E) → H*(F) (q-axis) and the map H*(B) → H*(E) induced by a section (p-axis), when a section exists.

Applications: The Serre spectral sequence computes H*(ΩX) from the path-loop fibration ΩX → PX → X, yielding the loop space homology. For the Hopf fibration S¹ → S³ → S², E₂p,q = Hᵖ(S²; H^q(S¹)) has nonzero entries only at (0,0), (0,1), (2,0), (2,1). The differential d₂: E₂0,1 → E₂2,0 must be an isomorphism for convergence to H*(S³) (which is ℤ in degrees 0 and 3). This identifies the generator of H²(S²) as the transgression of the generator of H¹(S¹). The Leray-Serre spectral sequence generalizes to homology with local coefficients when π₁(B) acts nontrivially on H*(F). The Eilenberg-Moore spectral sequence computes H*(F) from H*(E) and H*(B) when E → B is a principal fibration.

Characteristic Classes of Vector Bundles

Characteristic classes measure the twisting of a vector bundle ξ: E → B. They are cohomology classes in H*(B) that are natural under pullback: f* c(ξ) = c(f*ξ). For real vector bundles, the Stiefel-Whitney classes wᵢ(ξ) ∈ Hⁱ(B; ℤ/2) are defined via the Thom isomorphism and the Wu formula. The total Stiefel-Whitney class w = 1 + w₁ + w₂ + ... satisfies w(ξ ⊕ η) = w(ξ) ⌣ w(η) (Whitney sum formula). The first class w₁ detects orientability: w₁(ξ) = 0 iff ξ is orientable. The second class w₂ detects spin structures: a manifold is spin iff w₂(TM) = 0. For the tangent bundle of ℝPⁿ, w(TℝPⁿ) = (1 + a)ⁿ⁺¹ where a generates H¹(ℝPⁿ; ℤ/2).

For complex vector bundles, Chern classes cᵢ(ξ) ∈ H²ⁱ(B; ℤ) are defined via the splitting principle: any bundle splits as a sum of line bundles after pulling back to a suitable space (the flag bundle). The total Chern class c = 1 + c₁ + c₂ + ... satisfies c(ξ ⊕ η) = c(ξ) ⌣ c(η). The top Chern class cₙ(ξ) is the Euler class of the underlying real 2n-bundle. For the tautological line bundle γ over ℂPⁿ, c₁(γ) = -x where x generates H²(ℂPⁿ; ℤ). The tangent bundle of ℂPⁿ has c(TℂPⁿ) = (1 + x)ⁿ⁺¹. Pontryagin classes pᵢ(ξ) ∈ H⁴ⁱ(B; ℤ) for real bundles are defined by pᵢ(ξ) = (-1)ⁱ c₂ᵢ(ξ ⊗ ℂ). They capture the "complexified" twisting and appear in the Hirzebruch signature theorem: the signature of a 4k-manifold is ⟨Lₖ(p₁, ..., pₖ), [M]⟩ where Lₖ is the Hirzebruch L-polynomial.

The Euler class e(ξ) ∈ Hⁿ(B; ℤ) for an oriented rank-n bundle is the obstruction to a nowhere-zero section. It satisfies e(ξ ⊕ η) = e(ξ) ⌣ e(η) and e(ξ) = 0 if ξ has a nonvanishing section. For the tangent bundle of a closed oriented manifold M, ⟨e(TM), [M]⟩ = χ(M) (Poincaré-Hopf). The Thom isomorphism H*(B) ≅ H*+n(E, E₀) (where E₀ is the complement of the zero section) gives the Thom class u ∈ Hⁿ(E, E₀) whose restriction to each fiber is the generator. The Euler class is the pullback of u by the zero section. The Gysin sequence for a sphere bundle S(ξ) → B relates H*(B) and H*(S(ξ)) via the Euler class.

Obstruction Theory and Postnikov Towers

Obstruction theory answers the question: given a map f: A → Y defined on a subcomplex A ⊂ X, can it be extended to X? Assume Y is simple (π₁(Y) acts trivially on πₙ(Y)). Build the extension skeleton by skeleton. On the (n+1)-skeleton, the obstruction to extending over an (n+1)-cell en+1 is the homotopy class of f on ∂en+1 ≅ Sⁿ, giving a cochain in Cn+1(X, A; πₙ(Y)). This cochain is a cocycle; its cohomology class on+1(f) ∈ Hn+1(X, A; πₙ(Y)) is the primary obstruction. If it vanishes, the extension exists on the (n+1)-skeleton, and one proceeds to the next dimension. The obstructions depend on choices made at lower stages; the indeterminacy is captured by the action of lower obstructions.

For lifting problems: given p: E → B a fibration with fiber F, and f: X → B, a lift f̃: X → E exists iff the obstructions in Hn+1(X; πₙ(F)) vanish. The first obstruction to a section of a fiber bundle is the primary obstruction class. For the tangent bundle of a manifold, the obstruction to a nonvanishing vector field is the Euler class. For a map f: X → Y between CW complexes, the obstruction to a homotopy equivalence is captured by the Whitehead torsion in Wh(π₁(X)).

Postnikov towers systematize this. The n-th Postnikov section Pₙ(X) has πᵢ(Pₙ(X)) = πᵢ(X) for i ≤ n and 0 for i > n. The map X → Pₙ(X) induces isomorphisms on πᵢ for i ≤ n. The fiber of Pₙ(X) → Pₙ₋₁(X) is a K(πₙ(X), n). The k-invariant kₙ ∈ Hn+1(Pₙ₋₁(X); πₙ(X)) classifies this fibration. A map f: X → Y induces maps between Postnikov towers, and the obstructions to lifting f to a map of towers are the k-invariants. For simply connected spaces of finite type, the Postnikov tower with k-invariants is a complete invariant of the homotopy type. The minimal model in rational homotopy theory (Sullivan) replaces k-invariants with a commutative differential graded algebra, making rational homotopy types computable via algebraic models.

  • Confusing the Hurewicz homomorphism direction: it goes πₙ → Hₙ, not the reverse. The isomorphism πₙ ≅ Hₙ holds only under (n−1)-connectedness.
  • Forgetting that the Serre spectral sequence requires trivial π₁(B)-action on H*(F) for the simple E₂-page H*(B; H*(F)). With nontrivial action, use local coefficients.
  • Misapplying the Whitney sum formula: w(ξ ⊕ η) = w(ξ)w(η) uses cup product, not addition. The total class is multiplicative.
  • Thinking Chern classes live in odd degrees: cᵢ ∈ H²ⁱ(B; ℤ). Pontryagin classes pᵢ ∈ H⁴ⁱ(B; ℤ). Stiefel-Whitney classes wᵢ ∈ Hⁱ(B; ℤ/2).
  • Overlooking that obstruction theory requires simple coefficients (trivial π₁-action). For non-simple spaces, obstructions live in twisted cohomology or require covering space techniques.
  • Confusing the Euler class (obstruction to a section of an oriented vector bundle) with the top Stiefel-Whitney class (mod 2 Euler class). They agree mod 2 but differ integrally.
  • Assuming all fibrations are fiber bundles: Serre fibrations have the homotopy lifting property for CW complexes, which is weaker than local triviality. The path-loop fibration is a Serre fibration but not a fiber bundle.

K-Theory: Generalized Cohomology and Vector Bundle Classification

K-theory is a sophisticated generalized cohomology theory that provides powerful invariants for topological spaces, particularly in the context of vector bundles. Unlike ordinary cohomology, which is built from chain complexes of abelian groups, K-theory is constructed directly from the algebraic structure of vector bundles over a given space. The fundamental idea is to classify vector bundles up to stable isomorphism, meaning two bundles are considered equivalent if they become isomorphic after adding trivial bundles to both. This leads to the definition of the Grothendieck group K⁰(X) of a compact Hausdorff space X.

For a compact Hausdorff space X, K⁰(X) is defined as the Grothendieck group completion of the set of isomorphism classes of complex vector bundles over X, under the operation of Whitney sum. Intuitively, K⁰(X) captures the "difference" between vector bundles. Elements of K⁰(X) are formal differences [E] - [F] of vector bundle isomorphism classes. This construction endows K⁰(X) with the structure of a commutative ring, where multiplication is given by the tensor product of vector bundles. The group K¹(X) is defined as K⁰(SX), where SX is the suspension of X, or more generally, as the group of stable homotopy classes of maps from X to the space of Fredholm operators. Together, K⁰ and K¹ form the foundation of topological K-theory.

A cornerstone of K-theory is Bott periodicity, a remarkable theorem stating that K-theory is periodic with period two. Specifically, for a pointed space X, K̃ⁿ⁺²(X) ≅ K̃ⁿ(X), where K̃ denotes reduced K-theory. This means that all higher K-groups Kⁿ(X) can be expressed in terms of K⁰(X) and K¹(X). For complex K-theory, Kⁿ(X) ≅ K⁰(X) if n is even, and Kⁿ(X) ≅ K¹(X) if n is odd. For real K-theory, the periodicity is eight, leading to eight distinct K-groups. Bott periodicity is a deep result with profound implications, connecting K-theory to the topology of classical groups and providing a powerful computational tool.

K-theory is intimately related to characteristic classes. The Chern character, ch: K⁰(X) → H*(X; ℚ), is a natural transformation that maps K-theory classes to rational cohomology classes. It is a ring homomorphism and an isomorphism after tensoring with ℚ, making K-theory a "rationalized" version of cohomology in some sense. The Chern character allows us to translate information from K-theory into the more familiar language of cohomology, and vice-versa. For a line bundle L, ch(L) = ec₁(L) = 1 + c₁(L) + c₁(L)²/2! + ..., where c₁(L) is the first Chern class. For a general vector bundle E, ch(E) is defined via the splitting principle. The Todd class, Td(E), is another important characteristic class that appears in the context of the Riemann-Roch theorem and the Atiyah-Singer Index Theorem.

The Atiyah-Singer Index Theorem is one of the most celebrated results in 20th-century mathematics, linking topological invariants (K-theory classes, characteristic classes) to analytical invariants (the index of elliptic differential operators). It states that for an elliptic operator D on a compact manifold M, its analytical index (a number computed from the operator's properties) is equal to its topological index (a number computed from the characteristic classes of the manifold and the symbol of the operator, often involving K-theory). This theorem provides a powerful bridge between analysis, geometry, and topology, demonstrating the profound utility of K-theory in modern mathematics and physics, particularly in quantum field theory and string theory.

Bordism Theory: Classifying Manifolds Geometrically

Bordism theory offers a geometric approach to classifying manifolds, distinct from the algebraic invariants provided by homology or homotopy groups. Two closed n-dimensional manifolds M₀ and M₁ are said to be bordant if their disjoint union M₀ ⊔ M₁ forms the boundary of some (n+1)-dimensional manifold W. The manifold W is called a bordism between M₀ and M₁. If M₀ is bordant to M₁, we write M₀ ~ M₁. This relation is an equivalence relation, and the equivalence classes form an abelian group under disjoint union, known as the n-th bordism group, denoted Ωⁿ. Different versions of bordism exist depending on the additional structure imposed on the manifolds, such as orientability (oriented bordism ΩⁿSO), framed manifolds (framed bordism ΩⁿFr), or manifolds with a stable trivialization of their tangent bundle (stable bordism).

The structure of bordism groups is often complex, but they are deeply connected to other areas of algebraic topology. A fundamental result is Thom's theorem, which establishes an isomorphism between the oriented bordism groups ΩⁿSO and the stable homotopy groups of the Thom space MSO(n). More generally, for a given structure G (e.g., SO for oriented, O for unoriented, U for complex), the bordism groups ΩⁿG are isomorphic to the stable homotopy groups πⁿ(MG), where MG is the Thom spectrum associated with the structure G. This connection is crucial because it translates a geometric classification problem (bordism) into an algebraic one (stable homotopy groups), which can then be approached using spectral sequences and other tools of stable homotopy theory.

A key insight in bordism theory is that characteristic numbers are bordism invariants. For an oriented n-manifold M, its Pontryagin numbers (products of Pontryagin classes evaluated on the fundamental class [M]) and Euler characteristic are invariants. Similarly, for an unoriented n-manifold, its Stiefel-Whitney numbers (products of Stiefel-Whitney classes evaluated on the fundamental class [M]) are invariants. A fundamental theorem by René Thom states that two oriented manifolds are bordant if and only if all their Pontryagin numbers and their Euler characteristic agree. For unoriented manifolds, two manifolds are bordant if and only if all their Stiefel-Whitney numbers agree. This means that these characteristic numbers provide a complete set of invariants for classifying manifolds up to bordism.

The computation of bordism groups can be quite involved. For example, the unoriented bordism groups ΩⁿO were computed by Thom, showing that they are polynomial rings over ℤ/2 generated by classes of projective spaces. The oriented bordism groups ΩⁿSO are more complex, involving torsion and free parts. The study of bordism groups has led to significant advancements in understanding the structure of manifolds and their classification. For instance, the concept of bordism is central to the classification of exotic spheres (manifolds homeomorphic to standard spheres but not diffeomorphic), which are distinguished by their bordism properties and characteristic numbers. Bordism theory provides a powerful framework for understanding the global properties of manifolds by relating them to the boundaries of higher-dimensional manifolds, offering a unique perspective on geometric topology.

Computing Bordism Invariants
Consider the real projective plane ℙ²(ℝ). It is a closed, unoriented 2-manifold. Determine its Stiefel-Whitney numbers and explain their significance in unoriented bordism.
The Stiefel-Whitney classes wᵢ(M) ∈ Hⁱ(M; ℤ/2) are invariants of an unoriented manifold M. For ℙ²(ℝ), we know its cohomology ring H*(ℙ²(ℝ); ℤ/2) ≅ ℤ/2[x]/(x³), where x is the generator in H¹(ℙ²(ℝ); ℤ/2). The total Stiefel-Whitney class for ℙ²(ℝ) is given by w(ℙ²(ℝ)) = (1 + x)³. Expanding this, we get w(ℙ²(ℝ)) = 1 + 3x + 3x² + x³. Since we are in ℤ/2 cohomology, 3x = x and 3x² = x². So, w(ℙ²(ℝ)) = 1 + x + x² + x³. This implies:
  • w₀(ℙ²(ℝ)) = 1
  • w₁(ℙ²(ℝ)) = x ∈ H¹(ℙ²(ℝ); ℤ/2)
  • w₂(ℙ²(ℝ)) = x² ∈ H²(ℙ²(ℝ); ℤ/2)
  • w₃(ℙ²(ℝ)) = x³ = 0 (since H³(ℙ²(ℝ); ℤ/2) = 0)
The Stiefel-Whitney numbers are obtained by taking products of these classes such that the total degree is equal to the dimension of the manifold (n=2 in this case), and then evaluating on the fundamental class [ℙ²(ℝ)] ∈ H₂(ℙ²(ℝ); ℤ/2). The possible products are:
  1. w₁²: ⟨w₁(ℙ²(ℝ)) ⌣ w₁(ℙ²(ℝ)), [ℙ²(ℝ)]⟩ = ⟨x ⌣ x, [ℙ²(ℝ)]⟩ = ⟨x², [ℙ²(ℝ)]⟩. Since x² is the generator of H²(ℙ²(ℝ); ℤ/2), this pairing is 1.
  2. w₂: ⟨w₂(ℙ²(ℝ)), [ℙ²(ℝ)]⟩ = ⟨x², [ℙ²(ℝ)]⟩. This pairing is also 1.
Thus, the non-zero Stiefel-Whitney numbers for ℙ²(ℝ) are w₁² = 1 and w₂ = 1. Significance: According to Thom's theorem, two unoriented n-manifolds are bordant if and only if they have the same Stiefel-Whitney numbers. Since ℙ²(ℝ) has non-zero Stiefel-Whitney numbers, it is not bordant to the empty set (which has all numbers zero). This confirms that ℙ²(ℝ) is a non-bounding manifold in unoriented bordism. For instance, the torus T² has w(T²) = 1, so all its Stiefel-Whitney numbers are zero, meaning T² is bordant to the empty set (it bounds a solid torus). ℙ²(ℝ) is distinct from T² in unoriented bordism.
Distinguishing Generalized Cohomology Theories
When encountering generalized cohomology theories like K-theory or bordism, focus on their defining properties, the objects they classify (e.g., vector bundles for K-theory, manifolds for bordism), and their key theorems (e.g., Bott periodicity, Thom's theorem). Understand how they relate to ordinary cohomology (e.g., via Chern character or characteristic numbers). Examiners often test the conceptual understanding of what these theories measure and how their invariants are computed or interpreted.

Rational Homotopy Theory: Algebraic Models for Homotopy Types

Rational homotopy theory provides a powerful framework for studying the homotopy types of topological spaces by "rationalizing" their homotopy groups. This process simplifies the algebraic structure, often making previously intractable problems computable. The core idea is to tensor the homotopy groups πₙ(X) with the field of rational numbers ℚ, yielding the rational homotopy groups πₙ(X) ⊗ ℚ. This eliminates all torsion information, focusing solely on the free abelian group structure. For example, while π₃(S²) ≅ ℤ, π₃(S²) ⊗ ℚ ≅ ℚ. However, π₂(S²) ≅ ℤ, so π₂(S²) ⊗ ℚ ≅ ℚ. The challenge is to reconstruct the rational homotopy type from these rationalized groups.

There are two primary approaches to rational homotopy theory: Sullivan's theory of minimal models and Quillen's theory of Lie algebras. Sullivan's theory, developed in the 1970s, associates a commutative differential graded algebra (CDGA) to each simply connected topological space of finite type. A CDGA is an algebra A = ⨁ Aⁿ with a differential d: Aⁿ → Aⁿ⁺¹ satisfying d² = 0, d(ab) = d(a)b + (-1)deg(a)ad(b), and ab = (-1)deg(a)deg(b)ba. Sullivan's construction provides a functor from the category of simply connected spaces to the category of minimal CDGAs. A CDGA is "minimal" if it is free as an algebra and the differential acts decomposably. The cohomology of the minimal model is isomorphic to the rational cohomology of the space, H*(A, d) ≅ H*(X; ℚ). Crucially, the indecomposable elements of the minimal model are dual to the rational homotopy groups: πₙ(X) ⊗ ℚ ≅ (Aⁿ)∨.

Quillen's theory, developed concurrently, takes a dual approach. It associates a differential graded Lie algebra (DGLA) to each simply connected space. The homology of this DGLA is related to the rational homotopy groups. While Sullivan's models are more commonly used for computations, Quillen's models provide a deeper connection to algebraic structures like Lie algebras. The equivalence between these two approaches is a fundamental result, demonstrating the robustness of rational homotopy theory.

A space X is called rationally elliptic if its rational homotopy groups are finite-dimensional in each degree and its rational cohomology is finite-dimensional. Examples include spheres, complex projective spaces, and compact Lie groups. A space is rationally hyperbolic if its rational homotopy groups grow exponentially. The structure of minimal models for elliptic spaces is particularly constrained. A key application of rational homotopy theory is the classification of formal spaces. A space X is formal if its minimal model is quasi-isomorphic to its rational cohomology algebra (H*(X; ℚ), 0). This means that all the higher-order Whitehead products and Massey products vanish rationally, implying that the rational homotopy type is completely determined by its rational cohomology. Examples of formal spaces include compact Kähler manifolds, spheres, and Lie groups. Non-formal spaces exhibit more complex rational homotopy structures, often related to the existence of non-trivial Massey products.

Rational homotopy theory has found applications in various fields, including the study of loop spaces, classifying spaces, and the topology of manifolds. It simplifies the computation of rational homotopy groups, provides insights into the existence of certain maps, and helps distinguish spaces that are indistinguishable by rational homology alone. For instance, the rational homotopy type of a product of spheres is easily computed from the rational homotopy types of the individual spheres, which is a much harder problem in integral homotopy theory.

Morse Theory: Connecting Critical Points to Topology

Morse theory, developed by Marston Morse, is a powerful technique that bridges differential topology and algebraic topology. It provides a method to understand the topology of a smooth manifold by analyzing the critical points of a smooth function defined on it. The central idea is that the topology of a manifold can be built up by attaching "handles" at each critical point of a well-behaved function, and the homology groups of the manifold can be computed from the number of critical points of different types.

A smooth function f: M → ℝ on a manifold M is called a Morse function if all its critical points are non-degenerate. A critical point p is where the gradient ∇f(p) = 0. It is non-degenerate if the Hessian matrix of f at p (the matrix of second partial derivatives) is non-singular. The index of a non-degenerate critical point p is the number of negative eigenvalues of the Hessian matrix at p. This index corresponds to the dimension of the "unstable manifold" at p, which is the space of directions in which f decreases most rapidly.

The fundamental theorem of Morse theory states that if f is a Morse function on a compact manifold M, then the number of critical points of index k, denoted cₖ, is related to the Betti numbers bₖ(M) (the rank of the k-th homology group Hₖ(M; ℤ)) by the Morse inequalities: c₀ ≥ b₀ c₁ ≥ b₁ ... cₙ ≥ bₙ And the alternating sum of the number of critical points equals the Euler characteristic of the manifold: χ(M) = Σ (-1)ᵏ cₖ = Σ (-1)ᵏ bₖ(M).

These inequalities provide lower bounds for the number of critical points required for a given topology. If a manifold has a "perfect" Morse function, meaning cₖ = bₖ for all k, then the Morse inequalities become equalities, and the homology of the manifold is directly read off from the critical points. For example, a sphere Sⁿ has two critical points for a height function: a minimum (index 0) and a maximum (index n). Thus, c₀=1, cₙ=1, and all other cₖ=0. This perfectly matches the homology of Sⁿ: b₀=1, bₙ=1, and other bₖ=0, so χ(Sⁿ) = 1 + (-1)ⁿ.

Morse theory also provides a geometric way to understand the structure of manifolds through handle decompositions. If f is a Morse function on M, then M can be constructed by starting with a manifold with boundary Mᵃ = f⁻¹((-∞, a]) and attaching k-handles at each critical point of index k. Specifically, if f has a single critical point of index k at level c, then Mc+ε is obtained from Mc-ε by attaching a k-handle. This process allows one to visualize the manifold's structure and its homology in terms of these attached handles. For instance, a 2-torus can be constructed by attaching one 0-handle (a disk), two 1-handles (cylinders), and one 2-handle (a disk) in a specific way, corresponding to c₀=1, c₁=2, c₂=1, which matches its homology b₀=1, b₁=2, b₂=1.

Applications of Morse theory are widespread. It is used to prove the existence of geodesics on Riemannian manifolds, to study the topology of loop spaces, and to classify manifolds. For example, the h-cobordism theorem, a cornerstone of high-dimensional topology, relies heavily on Morse theory. It also forms the basis for Floer homology in symplectic geometry and plays a role in understanding the topology of algebraic varieties. The ability to translate topological questions into analytical ones about critical points makes Morse theory an indispensable tool in modern geometry and topology.

Common Traps in Advanced Algebraic Topology

  • Confusing K-theory with ordinary cohomology: K-theory is a generalized cohomology theory based on vector bundles, not chain complexes. While related via the Chern character, they are distinct.
  • Misapplying Bott periodicity: Remember it's period 2 for complex K-theory (Kⁿ ≅ Kⁿ⁺²) and period 8 for real K-theory.
  • Assuming all manifolds are bordant to the empty set: Only manifolds with vanishing characteristic numbers (Pontryagin for oriented, Stiefel-Whitney for unoriented) are bordant to the empty set.
  • Forgetting the "stable" aspect of bordism groups: Bordism groups are often isomorphic to stable homotopy groups of Thom spectra, emphasizing their connection to stable phenomena.
  • Ignoring the "rational" aspect in rational homotopy theory: Results apply to homotopy groups tensored with ℚ, not necessarily the integral homotopy groups, which retain torsion information.
  • Confusing Sullivan's CDGAs with Quillen's DGLAs: While dual, understand which algebraic structure corresponds to which approach and their respective strengths.
  • Misinterpreting the index of a Morse critical point: The index is the number of negative eigenvalues of the Hessian, not simply the dimension of the manifold or the critical point's "level".
  • Assuming all Morse functions are "perfect": While ideal for computation, many Morse functions are not perfect, and the Morse inequalities provide bounds, not necessarily equalities, for general functions.
  • Overlooking the non-degeneracy condition for Morse functions: A critical point must be non-degenerate for the index to be well-defined and for Morse theory to apply directly.

Model Categories and Homotopical Algebra

A model category provides an abstract framework for doing homotopy theory in any category that admits three distinguished classes of morphisms: weak equivalences, fibrations, and cofibrations. These classes satisfy axioms due to Quillen that guarantee the existence of a well‑behaved homotopy category obtained by formally inverting the weak equivalences. The axioms require that each class is closed under retracts, that the two‑out‑of‑three property holds for weak equivalences, and that lifting and factorisation properties hold: any morphism can be factored as a cofibration followed by a trivial fibration, and also as a trivial cofibration followed by a fibration. The canonical example is the category of topological spaces with Serre fibrations, Hurewicz cofibrations, and weak homotopy equivalences. Another fundamental example is the category of chain complexes of modules over a ring, where weak equivalences are quasi‑isomorphisms, fibrations are degreewise surjections, and cofibrations are degreewise injections with projective cokernels. Model categories allow one to define derived functors in a purely categorical way: the left derived functor of a left Quillen functor is computed by applying the functor to a cofibrant replacement, while the right derived functor of a right Quillen functor uses a fibrant replacement. This machinery underlies modern approaches to derived algebraic geometry, where one works with simplicial commutative rings or differential graded algebras equipped with model structures. The homotopy category of a model category carries a natural triangulated structure when the model category is stable, linking model categories to stable homotopy theory and derived categories. Understanding the interplay between the three classes of maps, the construction of (co)fibrant replacements, and the passage to the homotopy category is essential for any advanced work that uses homotopical methods beyond spaces.

Stable Homotopy Theory and Spectra

Stable homotopy theory studies phenomena that become invariant under suspension. The suspension functor Σ shifts homotopy groups: πₖ₊₁(ΣX) ≅ πₖ(X). A spectrum E is a sequence of pointed spaces {Eₙ} together with structure maps ΣEₙ → Eₙ₊₁. The stable homotopy groups of a space X are defined as πₖˢ(X) = colimₙ πₖ₊ₙ(ΣⁿX). Spectra form a stable model category (or ∞‑category) where the suspension functor is an equivalence. The sphere spectrum 𝕊, with 𝕊ₙ = Sⁿ, is the unit for the smash product of spectra, making the stable homotopy category a symmetric monoidal triangulated category. Important constructions include the Eilenberg–Mac Lane spectrum Hℤ representing ordinary cohomology, the complex K‑theory spectrum KU, and the complex cobordism spectrum MU. The Adams–Novikov spectral sequence, based on MU, and the classical Adams spectral sequence, based on the mod‑p Steenrod algebra, are the primary computational tools for stable homotopy groups of spheres. The nilpotence theorem of Devinatz–Hopkins–Smith and the thick subcategory theorem classify thick subcategories of finite spectra in terms of Morava K‑theories, leading to chromatic homotopy theory. In this framework, the height of a formal group law associated to a complex‑oriented theory measures its “chromatic level”. The sphere spectrum sits at height 0, KU at height 1, and Morava E‑theories at higher heights. Understanding spectra, smash products, and the stable homotopy category is indispensable for modern calculations and for the conceptual organisation of generalized cohomology theories.

Worked Example: Computing π₁ˢ(S⁰) via the Adams Spectral Sequence
Use the classical Adams spectral sequence at the prime 2 to determine the first stable stem π₁ˢ(S⁰).
The E₂‑page of the Adams spectral sequence is Ext𝒜s,t(H⁎(S⁰;𝔽₂), H⁎(S⁰;𝔽₂)) where 𝒜 is the mod‑2 Steenrod algebra. Since H⁎(S⁰;𝔽₂) = 𝔽₂ concentrated in degree 0, the Ext groups reduce to Ext𝒜s,t(𝔽₂, 𝔽₂). The (s,t)‑bigrading gives a spectral sequence converging to the 2‑completed stable homotopy groups πt‑sˢ(S⁰)̂₂. In the range t‑s ≤ 1 the only non‑zero Ext classes are: Ext0,0 = 𝔽₂ (the unit), Ext1,1 = 𝔽₂ generated by h₀, and Ext1,2 = 𝔽₂ generated by h₁. The differential d₂(h₁) = h₀² = 0 because h₀² = 0 in the Steenrod algebra. No further differentials can hit these classes. Thus the E∞‑page yields a filtration on π₀ˢ ≅ ℤ₂̂ (the 2‑adic integers) and π₁ˢ ≅ ℤ/2 generated by the class η corresponding to h₁. Therefore π₁ˢ(S⁰) ≅ ℤ/2, represented by the Hopf map η: S³ → S² after one suspension.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks for a stable homotopy group, first identify the prime and the range. Write down the relevant Ext chart (Adams or Adams–Novikov) up to the required stem. Remember that the sphere spectrum is the unit for the smash product, so any ring spectrum gives a module over the stable homotopy groups of spheres. Use the nilpotence theorem to argue that elements detected by Morava K‑theories are nilpotent unless they are units. This conceptual shortcut often replaces lengthy spectral sequence calculations.

Adams Spectral Sequence and Computation of Stable Homotopy Groups

The Adams spectral sequence is a first‑quadrant spectral sequence of the form E₂s,t = Ext𝒜s,t(H⁎(Y;𝔽ₚ), H⁎(X;𝔽ₚ)) ⇒ πt‑s(X)̂ₚ, where 𝒜 is the mod‑p Steenrod algebra, X and Y are spectra, and the target is the p‑completion of the stable homotopy groups of the function spectrum F(Y,X). For the sphere spectrum X = Y = 𝕊, the E₂‑page is Ext𝒜s,t(𝔽ₚ, 𝔽ₚ). The algebra structure of 𝒜 is known: at p = 2 it is generated by Steenrod squares Sqⁱ subject to Adem relations; at odd p it is generated by the Bockstein β and reduced powers Pⁱ. The Ext groups have been computed up to a high stem using the May spectral sequence and computer algebra. Key permanent cycles include h₀ (detecting 2), h₁ (detecting η), h₂ (detecting ν), and h₃ (detecting σ). The differentials are often determined by Massey products in Ext or by geometric arguments such as the Toda bracket. For example, d₂(h₄) = h₀h₃² in the 2‑primary Adams spectral sequence, which kills the would‑be element in stem 30. The Adams–Novikov spectral sequence replaces 𝒜 with the Hopf algebroid (MU⁎, MU⁎MU) and converges to the same completed homotopy groups but with better algebraic structure; its E₂‑page is ExtMU⁎MUs,t(MU⁎, MU⁎). This spectral sequence is the engine of chromatic homotopy theory: the filtration by height corresponds to the filtration by Morava K‑theories K(n). The nilpotence theorem implies that any element in the stable homotopy groups of spheres that is nilpotent in the K(n)‑local category is globally nilpotent. Consequently, the chromatic convergence theorem reconstructs the p‑completed sphere from its K(n)‑localisations. Mastery of the Adams and Adams–Novikov spectral sequences, including the ability to read Ext charts and to identify differentials via known patterns, is a hallmark of advanced stable homotopy theory.

Equivariant Cohomology and the Borel Construction

Equivariant cohomology extends ordinary cohomology to spaces with a group action. For a topological group G acting on a space X, the Borel construction replaces X by the homotopy quotient X_G = EG ×_G X, where EG is a contractible space with a free G‑action (the universal principal G‑bundle). The equivariant cohomology is then defined as H_G⁎(X; R) = H⁎(X_G; R) for a coefficient ring R. When G is a compact Lie group, EG can be taken as the infinite join of copies of G, and X_G is the total space of a fibration X → X_G → BG with fibre X. The Serre spectral sequence of this fibration computes H_G⁎(X) from the action of π₁(BG) on H⁎(X). If the action is trivial on cohomology, the E₂‑page is H⁎(BG; H⁎(X)) and the spectral sequence collapses at E₂ under favourable conditions (e.g., X a point, giving H_G⁎(pt) = H⁎(BG)). For a torus T = (S¹)ⁿ, BT = (ℂP^∞)ⁿ and H_T⁎(pt) = ℤ[x₁,…,xₙ] with |xᵢ| = 2. The localisation theorem in equivariant cohomology states that after inverting the equivariant Euler classes of the non‑trivial representations, the inclusion of the fixed‑point set X^G → X induces an isomorphism. This is the algebraic topology analogue of the Atiyah‑Bott‑Berline‑Vergne localisation formula in equivariant integration. Equivariant K‑theory K_G⁎(X) is defined analogously using the Borel construction or, more geometrically, as the Grothendieck group of G‑equivariant vector bundles. The Atiyah‑Segal completion theorem relates K_G⁎(X) completed at the augmentation ideal to K⁎(X_G). Equivariant cohomology plays a central role in the study of Hamiltonian group actions, symplectic reduction, and the topology of moduli spaces. It also underlies the definition of equivariant characteristic classes such as the equivariant Chern and Pontryagin classes, which live in H_G⁎(X).

Intersection Cohomology and Poincaré Duality for Singular Spaces

Intersection cohomology, introduced by Goresky and MacPherson, restores Poincaré duality for singular algebraic or topological spaces. For a stratified pseudomanifold X of dimension n, one defines a perversity function p̄ assigning to each stratum of codimension c an integer p̄(c) satisfying p̄(2) = 0 and p̄(c) ≤ p̄(c+1) ≤ p̄(c)+1. The intersection chain complex IC̄_p̄(X) consists of chains that meet strata of codimension c in dimension at most dim(chain) − c + p̄(c). Its homology groups IH̄_p̄^k(X) are the intersection homology groups; cohomology is defined by dualising. The two extreme perversities are the zero perversity p̄₀(c)=0 (giving ordinary homology for a manifold) and the top perversity p̄_t(c)=c−2 (giving Borel–Moore homology). The fundamental theorem states that for any perversity p̄, there is a Poincaré duality isomorphism IH̄_p̄^k(X) ≅ IH̄p̄^∨n−k(X) where p̄^∨ is the complementary perversity p̄^∨(c) = c−2−p̄(c). In particular, middle perversities (lower middle m̄(c)=⌊(c−2)/2⌋ and upper middle n̄(c)=⌈(c−2)/2⌉) are self‑dual up to a shift. Intersection cohomology carries a pure Hodge structure when X is a complex projective variety, and the decomposition theorem of Beilinson‑Bernstein‑Deligne‑Gabber asserts that the pushforward of the intersection complex under a proper map splits into a direct sum of shifted intersection complexes of subvarieties. This theorem underlies many results in the topology of algebraic maps, including the hard Lefschetz theorem for singular varieties. Intersection cohomology also appears in the geometric Langlands program and in the study of character sheaves. For exam purposes, one should be able to state the axioms of a perversity, describe the allowable chain conditions, and explain why ordinary homology fails Poincaré duality on a cone over a manifold (the cone point creates a stratum of codimension n+1 where the link has non‑trivial homology). The intersection cohomology of the cone is trivial in middle degrees, restoring duality.

  • Confusing the Borel construction with the actual quotient X/G: the Borel construction is homotopy invariant, while X/G can have terrible homotopy type.
  • Assuming the Adams spectral sequence always collapses at E₂: many differentials are non‑trivial and must be identified using Massey products or geometric input.
  • Treating the Steenrod algebra as commutative: it is a non‑commutative Hopf algebra; Ext computations require careful handling of the algebra structure.
  • Forgetting that intersection cohomology depends on the choice of perversity: different perversities give different groups, and only complementary perversities are dual.
  • Misapplying the localisation theorem without inverting the appropriate Euler classes: the isomorphism holds only after localisation.
  • Confusing stable homotopy groups of spheres with unstable homotopy groups: suspension is an isomorphism only in the stable range (Freudenthal suspension theorem).
  • Neglecting the completion step in the Adams spectral sequence: the target is the p‑completed homotopy groups, not the integral groups.

Chromatic Homotopy Theory and Morava K-Theories

Chromatic homotopy theory organises the stable homotopy category by the height of formal groups associated to complex-oriented cohomology theories. A complex-oriented ring spectrum E gives a formal group law over π₀E via the tensor product of line bundles; the height of this formal group at a prime p measures the complexity of the theory. The Morava K-theories K(n) for n ≥ 0 are the fields of this classification: K(0) = HQ, K(1) is p-completed complex K-theory, and for n ≥ 2, K(n) has coefficient ring 𝔽ₚ[vₙ^±¹] with |vₙ| = 2(pⁿ−1). Each K(n) is a field in the stable homotopy category — every module is free — and the Künneth isomorphism holds. The telescope conjecture (open for n ≥ 2) predicts that the Bousfield class of K(n) equals that of the telescope of a vₙ-self-map on a finite spectrum. The chromatic tower arises from the Bousfield localisation functors Lₙ with respect to E(n) = ⋁ᵢ≤ₙ K(i); the fibre of Lₙ → Lₙ₋₁ is the monochromatic layer Mₙ = LK(n). The chromatic convergence theorem of Ravenel states that for a finite spectrum X, the natural map X → holimₙ LₙX is an isomorphism after p-completion. Lubin-Tate theory provides the universal deformation ring of a height-n formal group over 𝔽ₚ, yielding the Morava E-theory Eₙ with π₀Eₙ = W(𝔽ₚⁿ)[[u₁,…,uₙ₋₁]] and π₋₂Eₙ = u⁻¹π₀Eₙ. The Morava stabiliser group 𝔾ₙ = Aut(Γₙ) acts on Eₙ, and the homotopy fixed points Eₙh𝔾ₙ recover the K(n)-local sphere. The Adams-Novikov spectral sequence based on Eₙ computes πₖLK(n)S⁰; its E₂-page is continuous group cohomology H^s_c(𝔾ₙ; (Eₙ)_t). For n=1 this recovers the classical Adams spectral sequence for p-completed K-theory; for n=2 the cohomology of 𝔾₂ was computed by Shimomura and others, giving πₖLK(2)S⁰ for k up to several hundred. The chromatic red-shift phenomenon — that the height of the formal group increases under algebraic K-theory — conjecturally relates K(ku) to height 2 and K(ℓ) to height 3, where ℓ is the Adams summand.

Goodwillie Calculus and the Calculus of Functors

Goodwillie calculus provides a systematic method to approximate homotopy functors F: 𝒞 → 𝒟 (where 𝒞, 𝒟 are suitable model categories, e.g., spaces or spectra) by a tower of polynomial functors PₙF, analogous to the Taylor series of a smooth function. A functor is n-excisive if it sends strongly cocartesian (n+1)-cubes to cartesian cubes; the n-excisive approximation PₙF is the universal n-excisive functor under F. The Taylor tower F → ⋯ → PₙF → Pₙ₋₁F → ⋯ → P₀F has homogeneous layers DₙF = hofib(PₙF → Pₙ₋₁F) which are n-homogeneous: DₙF(∨ᵢ Xᵢ) ≃ ∏ᵢ DₙF(Xᵢ) and DₙF(ΣX) ≃ ΣⁿDₙF(X). Each DₙF is determined by its value on spheres and the action of the symmetric group Σₙ: DₙF(X) ≃ (∂ₙF ∧ X∧n)hΣₙ where ∂ₙF is the n-th derivative, a spectrum with Σₙ-action. For the identity functor on spaces, ∂ₙ(id) = S-n with trivial Σₙ-action, so Dₙ(id)(X) ≃ (X∧n)hΣₙ, the n-th extended power. The Goodwillie tower of the identity converges for simply connected spaces (and more generally for nilpotent spaces of finite type). For the functor Σ^∞Ω^∞: Sp → Sp, the derivatives are ∂ₙ = S with trivial Σₙ-action, giving the stable splitting of Σ^∞Ω^∞X ≃ ∨ₙ (X∧n)hΣₙ. The calculus of functors extends to functors of several variables (multivariable Goodwillie calculus) and to parametric versions. The chain rule holds: ∂ₙ(G∘F) ≃ ⊕k (∂ₖG ∧ (∂F)∧k)hΣₖ with appropriate Σₙ-action. Applications include the computation of the homotopy groups of diffeomorphism groups via embedding calculus (Weiss), the study of the K-theory of spaces via the trace map to topological Hochschild homology, and the analysis of the homotopy type of mapping spaces. The convergence of the Taylor tower for the identity on spaces is equivalent to the convergence of the EHP sequence; the layers Dₙ(id)(Sᵏ) are related to the homotopy groups of spheres via the Freudenthal suspension theorem.

Worked Computation: Derivative of the Identity
Compute the first derivative ∂₁(id) of the identity functor on based spaces and verify that the linear approximation P₁(id)(X) is the suspension spectrum Σ^∞X.
The first derivative ∂₁F of a functor F is defined as the spectrum representing the functor X ↦ hofib(F(X) → P₀F(X)) on finite spectra. For F = id, P₀F is the constant functor at the basepoint, so hofib(id(X) → *) = X. The representing spectrum is the sphere spectrum S, but we must account for the shift: ∂₁F is defined so that D₁F(X) ≃ (∂₁F ∧ X)hΣ₁ = ∂₁F ∧ X. Since D₁(id)(X) = hofib(X → *) = X, we need ∂₁(id) ∧ X ≃ X for all X, hence ∂₁(id) ≃ S. However, the standard indexing in Goodwillie's papers places ∂ₙ in degree -n, so ∂₁(id) = S-1 = Σ-1S. Then D₁(id)(X) = (S-1 ∧ X)hΣ₁ = Σ-1X. But the linear approximation P₁(id) is not D₁; it is the fibre of id → P₀, which is id itself on connected spaces. The correct statement: the linearisation of the identity on spaces is the functor X ↦ Ω^∞Σ^∞X, whose associated spectrum is Σ^∞X. The derivative ∂₁(id) = S-1 gives D₁(id)(X) = Σ-1X, and the tower for the identity on spaces has P₁(id)(X) = X for connected X, with the map P₁ → P₀ being the collapse to a point. The suspension spectrum appears when we consider the functor Σ^∞: Spaces → Spectra; its first derivative is S, and P₁(Σ^∞)(X) = Σ^∞X.
Exam Strategy: Recognising Goodwillie Towers
When a question asks for the "Taylor tower" or "polynomial approximation" of a homotopy functor, identify the excisivity degree. The identity on spaces is 1-excisive on connected spaces (P₁ = id). The functor Σ^∞Ω^∞ on spectra is analytic with radius ∞. The functor K(-) (algebraic K-theory) has P₁K(X) = K(ℤ) × BGL(ℤ)+ for connected X. Memorise the derivatives of the identity (∂ₙ = S-n) and of Σ^∞Ω^∞ (∂ₙ = S). For a composite G∘F, use the chain rule: the n-th derivative is a sum over partitions of n of smash products of derivatives of G and F. In multiple-choice questions, the homogeneous layer DₙF is always of the form (∂ₙF ∧ X∧n)hΣₙ — check the symmetric group action and the smash power.

Topological Modular Forms and Elliptic Cohomology

Topological modular forms (TMF) is a connective spectrum whose homotopy groups are built from the ring of integral modular forms, providing a topological refinement of elliptic cohomology. The construction begins with the moduli stack Mell of elliptic curves over Spec ℤ, compactified to M̄ell by adding nodal cubics. The Goerss-Hopkins-Miller theorem produces a sheaf of E_∞ ring spectra Otop on M̄ell (in the étale topology) whose global sections are TMF = Γ(M̄ell, Otop). The homotopy groups πₖTMF are computable via the descent spectral sequence E₂s,t = H^s(M̄ell; ω⊗t/2) ⇒ πt-sTMF, where ω is the line bundle of invariant differentials. The E₂-page is the cohomology of M̄ell with coefficients in powers of ω; for t odd the cohomology vanishes. The ring of modular forms MF^* = ⊕ₖ H⁰(M̄ell; ω⊗k) maps to π2kTMF via the edge homomorphism; the cusp form Δ ∈ MF12 maps to an element in π24TMF which is not invertible (TMF is connective). Inverting Δ gives the periodic version TMF[Δ-1] ≃ tmf, the non-connective topological modular forms, whose homotopy groups are the ring of modular forms over ℤ with Δ inverted. The string orientation σ: MString → TMF is a map of E_∞ ring spectra constructed by Ando-Hopkins-Rezk-Strickland; it refines the Witten genus, a genus MString_* → MF^* taking values in modular forms. The Witten genus of a string manifold vanishes if the manifold admits a metric of positive Ricci curvature (Stolz conjecture, proved in many cases). The homotopy groups of TMF have been computed through a range: πₖTMF for k ≤ 60 are known explicitly and exhibit rich torsion (2, 3, and 5-primary). At p=2, TMF(2) splits as a sum of copies of BP⟨2⟩ (the truncated Brown-Peterson spectrum at height 2); at p=3, it relates to the Morava E-theory E₂. The Adams-Novikov spectral sequence for TMF has E₂-page ExtΓ(MF^*, MF^*) where Γ is the Hopf algebroid of modular forms; this was used by Behrens and others to compute πₖTMF. TMF also carries an action of the group SL₂(ℤ) via the modular group action on Mell, and the homotopy fixed points under the congruence subgroup Γ₀(p) give the spectra TMF₀(p) used in the study of the K(2)-local sphere.

Factorization Homology and Higher Algebra

Factorization homology (or topological chiral homology) ∫_M A assigns to a framed n-manifold M and an E_n-algebra A in a symmetric monoidal ∞-category 𝒞 a value in 𝒞, generalising the Hochschild homology of an associative algebra (E₁) and the factorisation homology of a commutative algebra (E_∞). For an E_n-algebra A in spectra, ∫ℝⁿ A ≃ A, and for a disjoint union ∫M ⊔ N A ≃ ∫_M A ⊗ ∫_N A. The theory satisfies excision: if M = U ∪ V with U ∩ V a collar, then ∫_M A is the pushout of ∫_U A ← ∫U∩V A → ∫_V A. When M is a closed n-manifold, ∫_M A is the factorization homology of A over M. For n=1, an E₁-algebra is an associative algebra; ∫S¹ A ≃ HH(A) (Hochschild homology), and ∫[0,1] A ≃ A as an (A,A)-bimodule. For n=2, an E₂-algebra is a braided monoidal category (in Cat) or a Gerstenhaber algebra (in chain complexes); ∫T² A computes the Hochschild homology of the Drinfeld centre. The cobordism hypothesis (Baez-Dolan, Lurie) states that the ∞-category of fully extended n-dimensional topological field theories with values in 𝒞 is equivalent to the ∞-category of fully dualisable objects in 𝒞. An E_n-algebra A gives an n-dimensional TFT Z_A with Z_A(pt) = A; the value on a closed n-manifold M is ∫_M A. For E_∞-algebras (commutative algebras), factorization homology over M is the derived global sections of the constant sheaf A on the Ran space of M. The higher algebra framework (Lurie's Higher Algebra) provides the ∞-operads E_n, the ∞-categories of E_n-algebras, and the bar construction BarE_n(A) which computes factorization homology via a simplicial object. The Dunn additivity theorem states that En+m ≃ E_n ⊗ E_m (Boardman-Vogt tensor product), so an En+m-algebra is an E_n-algebra in E_m-algebras. This underlies the iterated loop space recognition: an n-fold loop space is an E_n-space, and the group completion ΩⁿΣⁿX → ΩⁿΣⁿX+ is the universal map to a grouplike E_n-space. Factorization homology of the E_n-algebra Σ^∞_+ ΩⁿΣⁿX over M recovers the stable homotopy type of the mapping space Map(M, ΣⁿX) when M is a framed n-manifold.

Topological Cyclic Homology and the Cyclotomic Trace

Topological cyclic homology TC is a cyclotomic spectrum that approximates algebraic K-theory via the cyclotomic trace map K → TC. For a ring spectrum R, the topological Hochschild homology THH(R) is the factorization homology ∫S¹ R (with R viewed as an E_∞-algebra). THH(R) carries a natural S¹-action and, crucially, a cyclotomic structure: for each prime p, a map THH(R) → THH(R)tC_p (Tate construction) compatible with the S¹-action. The cyclotomic trace K(R) → THH(R) factors through TC(R), defined as the homotopy limit of the diagram ⋯ → THH(R)hCpⁿ → THH(R)hCpn-1 → ⋯ → THH(R)hS¹ where the maps are the Frobenius and restriction. Equivalently, TC(R) = THH(R)hS¹ ×THH(R)tS¹ THH(R)hS¹ (the fibre product over the Tate construction). The fundamental theorem of Bökstedt-Hsiang-Madsen (proved in full generality by Dundas-Goodwillie-McCarthy and Nikolaus-Scholze) states that for a connective ring spectrum R, the cyclotomic trace induces an equivalence on p-completions after inverting p: K(R)^∧_p ≃ TC(R)^∧_p. For discrete rings, this gives a practical method to compute K-theory: TC(ℤ) has been computed (Hesselholt-Madsen), yielding K(ℤ)^∧_p. The homotopy groups π_*TC(ℤ) are known explicitly: π₀ = ℤ_p, π-1 = 0, π2k = ℤ_p for k ≥ 1, π2k-1 = ℤ/p^ν_p(k) for k ≥ 1, where ν_p is the p-adic valuation. For a smooth algebra A over a perfect field k of characteristic p, TC(A) relates to the de Rham-Witt complex W_Ω^*_A via the Hesselholt-Madsen isomorphism π_*TC(A) ≅ W_Ω^*A (with a degree shift). The cyclotomic trace also exists for ∞-categories: for a stable ∞-category 𝒞, K(𝒞) → TC(𝒞) is an equivalence on p-completions if 𝒞 is "smooth and proper" (Blumberg-Gepner-Tabuada). This underlies the computation of K-theory of schemes via TC of perfect complexes. The cyclotomic structure on THH is encoded by the action of the cyclotomic category Λ (Connes' cyclic category) or, more modernly, by the structure of a cyclotomic spectrum in the sense of Nikolaus-Scholze: a spectrum with S¹-action and Frobenius maps φ_p: X → XtC_p satisfying compatibility conditions. The category of cyclotomic spectra admits a symmetric monoidal structure, and TC is the right adjoint to the inclusion of cyclotomic spectra into spectra with S¹-action.

  • Confusing the chromatic height of a formal group with the Morava K-theory index: K(n) has height n, but E(n) = ⋁i≤n K(i) detects height ≤ n; the telescope conjecture concerns whether LK(n) = LT(n) where T(n) is the telescope of a v_n-map.
  • Assuming the Goodwillie tower always converges: convergence requires the functor to be analytic (radius of convergence > 0) and the input to be sufficiently connected; the identity on spaces converges for simply connected spaces but not for arbitrary spaces.
  • Treating TMF as a periodic theory: TMF is connective; the periodic version is tmf = TMF[Δ-1]. The string orientation lands in TMF, not tmf; the Witten genus takes values in MF^* which maps to π_*TMF but the map is not injective.
  • Misidentifying the derivative of a functor: ∂ₙF is a spectrum with Σₙ-action, not merely a spectrum. The symmetric group action is essential for the formula DₙF(X) ≃ (∂ₙF ∧ X∧n)hΣₙ.
  • Overlooking the cyclotomic Frobenius in TC: TC is not simply the homotopy fixed points THHhS¹; the Tate construction and the Frobenius maps φ_p are essential. The equivalence K ≃ TC after p-completion uses the full cyclotomic structure, not just the S¹-action.
  • Confusing factorization homology with ordinary homology: ∫_M A depends on the E_n-structure of A and the framing of M; for E_∞-algebras it reduces to derived global sections, but for E₁ it gives Hochschild homology which is not the homology of M.
  • Assuming the Adams-Novikov spectral sequence for TMF collapses at E₂: it does not; differentials are non-trivial and are computed using the geometry of the moduli stack and the action of the Steenrod algebra on modular forms.

The String Orientation of TMF and the Witten Genus

The string orientation is a map of ring spectra MString → TMF that refines the classical Witten genus. The Witten genus assigns to a string manifold M a modular form of weight dim M/2 with integral coefficients, constructed from the Dirac operator on the loop space LM. Topologically, the genus factors through the homotopy groups of the connective spectrum TMF (topological modular forms). The orientation is built by first constructing a map MString → MO⟨8⟩ killing the first two Stiefel‑Whitney classes and the first fractional Pontryagin class, then using the Goerss‑Hopkins‑Miller theorem to lift the elliptic curve universal deformation to a sheaf of E∞‑ring spectra over the compactified moduli stack M̄ell. The resulting map on homotopy groups sends the string bordism class of a manifold to the q‑expansion of its Witten genus. Crucially, the map is not an equivalence; its image consists of those modular forms that are integral at the cusp and satisfy the divisibility conditions coming from the string condition. The kernel contains torsion elements detected by the Adams‑Novikov spectral sequence at primes 2 and 3. The orientation has been used to compute the string bordism groups in low degrees and to prove that the Witten genus of a string manifold with positive Ricci curvature vanishes, a topological analogue of the Lichnerowicz theorem.

Construction via the sigma orientation

An intermediate step is the sigma orientation MString → tmf where tmf is the periodic version of TMF. The sigma orientation is built from the universal elliptic curve with level structure and the associated formal group. The map on homotopy groups is given by the q‑expansion principle: a class in π*MString maps to a modular form whose q‑expansion is the index of a Dirac operator on the loop space. The sigma orientation is a map of E∞‑ring spectra, hence respects power operations. This compatibility forces the image to lie in the subring of modular forms invariant under the action of the Hecke operators at primes of good reduction.

Applications to positive curvature

Stolz and Teichner conjectured that a string manifold admitting a metric of positive Ricci curvature has vanishing Witten genus. The string orientation provides a homotopy‑theoretic proof for simply connected manifolds of dimension ≤ 7: the relevant homotopy groups of TMF are torsion‑free in those degrees, so the vanishing of the genus in π*TMF forces the string bordism class to be zero. In higher dimensions the argument requires analysis of the Adams‑Novikov differentials on the image of the orientation, which are known to be non‑trivial at the prime 2.

The Telescope Conjecture and Chromatic Splitting

The telescope conjecture, formulated by Ravenel, asserts that for each n ≥ 1 the Bousfield localization LK(n) with respect to the Morava K‑theory K(n) coincides with the localization LT(n) with respect to the telescope of a vn‑self map on a finite type n spectrum. Here T(n) = colim(vn−k) is the mapping telescope. The conjecture is known to hold for n = 1 (by Miller’s theorem on the localization at K(1) ≃ KUp) and for n = 2 at the prime 3 (by work of Behrens, Hill, Hopkins, and Mahowald). For n ≥ 2 at the prime 2 it remains open and is widely believed to be false. A counterexample would produce a spectrum that is T(n)‑local but not K(n)‑local, implying the existence of exotic K(n)‑local spheres. The chromatic splitting conjecture, due to Hovey and Ravenel, predicts a decomposition of the K(n)‑local sphere after K(n−1)‑localization: LK(n−1)LK(n)S0 ≃ LK(n−1)S0 ∨ Σ−1LK(n−1)LK(n)S0. This splitting would give a recursive computation of the K(n)‑local stable homotopy groups. Both conjectures are deeply tied to the structure of the Morava stabilizer group Gn and the action of the Adams‑Novikov spectral sequence on the Lubin‑Tate tower.

Known results and partial progress

At height 2 and prime 3, the telescope conjecture holds because the Morava stabilizer group has a finite subgroup of order 3 that forces the v2‑map to be central in the K(2)‑local category. At height 2 and prime 2, the conjecture is equivalent to the statement that the K(2)‑local sphere is built from a finite complex of type 2, which is not known. The chromatic splitting conjecture has been verified for n = 1 (trivial) and for n = 2 at primes ≥ 5 using the theory of topological modular forms and the geometry of the moduli stack of formal groups. The splitting fails at the prime 2 for n = 2 because of exotic torsion in π−1LK(2)S0 detected by the Mahowald invariant.

Implications for the stable homotopy category

If the telescope conjecture fails, the Bousfield lattice of the stable homotopy category contains more than the known chromatic localizations; there would be intermediate localizing subcategories between LT(n) and LK(n). This would affect the classification of thick subcategories of finite spectra and the structure of the Picard group of the K(n)‑local category. Conversely, a proof of chromatic splitting would give an inductive algorithm for computing π*LK(n)S0 in terms of the cohomology of the Morava stabilizer group, making the chromatic filtration completely computable in principle.

Worked Example: Detecting a v2‑map on a Type‑2 Complex
Let X be a finite spectrum of type 2 at the prime 3 (e.g. the cofiber of a v1‑map on a type‑1 complex). Explain how to construct a v2‑self map v2 : ΣdX → X and why its telescope T(2) = colim(v2−k) is K(2)‑local.
By the periodicity theorem of Hopkins‑Smith, any finite type‑n spectrum admits a vn‑self map. For X of type 2, choose a map v2 inducing multiplication by a unit in π*K(2)∧X. The cofiber of v2 is K(2)‑acyclic, so the telescope T(2) is K(2)‑local because K(2)∧T(2) ≃ K(2)∧X[v2−1] is a module over the field K(2)∧K(2). Since K(2) is a field spectrum, any K(2)‑local spectrum is a module over K(2), and the telescope construction inverts v2 which acts as an isomorphism on K(2)‑homology. Hence T(2) is K(2)‑local. The telescope conjecture at height 2 and prime 3 asserts that every K(2)‑local spectrum is already T(2)‑local; this is known to be true by the work of Behrens et al.
Exam Strategy for Chromatic Conjectures
When a question asks about the telescope conjecture, first state the precise equivalence LK(n) = LT(n). Then list the known cases: n=1 all primes, n=2 at p=3, open otherwise. Mention the connection to the chromatic splitting conjecture and the role of the Morava stabilizer group. For a counterexample sketch, describe a hypothetical T(n)‑local spectrum with non‑trivial K(n−1)‑homology. Always cite the relevant authors (Ravenel, Hovey, Behrens‑Hill‑Hopkins‑Mahowald) to show depth.

The Mahowald Invariant and the Image of J

The Mahowald invariant is a homomorphism M : π*S0 → π*+1S0 defined on the 2‑primary stable stems. It sends a class detected by a permanent cycle in the Adams spectral sequence at filtration s to a class detected at filtration s+1, effectively raising the Adams filtration by one. The invariant is constructed using the geometry of the stunted real projective spaces RP∞/RPn and the action of the Steenrod algebra on their cohomology. Mahowald showed that the image of M coincides with the subgroup of the stable stems generated by the image of the J‑homomorphism J : π*(O) → π*S0 after 2‑completion. The J‑homomorphism maps the homotopy groups of the orthogonal group into the stable stems; its image is a direct summand of the 2‑primary part of π*S0 and is completely known by Adams’ work: it consists of cyclic groups of order 2ν(k)+1 in degrees 8k−1 and 8k+1, where ν(k) is the 2‑adic valuation of k. The Mahowald invariant provides a systematic way to produce these classes from lower‑filtration elements, and it extends to a map on the Adams‑Novikov E₂‑page that respects the chromatic filtration.

Construction via the Adams spectral sequence

In the classical Adams spectral sequence at p=2, the E₂‑page is ExtA(F₂, F₂) where A is the Steenrod algebra. The Mahowald invariant corresponds to multiplication by the element h₀ in Ext, which raises the stem by one and the filtration by one. However, h₀‑multiplication is not always injective on permanent cycles; the invariant is defined on the quotient by the kernel of h₀. Mahowald proved that for any class x in the 2‑primary stable stems, there exists a unique class M(x) such that h₀·x = M(x) in the E∞‑page, provided x is not in the image of h₀. This yields a well‑defined homomorphism on the stable stems modulo the image of h₀.

Relation to the image of J and to tmf

The image of J is detected in the Adams spectral sequence by the classes h₁, h₂, … in ExtA(F₂, F₂). Mahowald showed that applying the invariant repeatedly to the generator h₁ produces the entire image of J. Moreover, the Mahowald invariant is compatible with the map of ring spectra S0 → tmf; under this map the invariant corresponds to the action of the Hecke operator T₂ on modular forms. This connection explains why the image of J maps to torsion‑free classes in π*tmf and why the Mahowald invariant vanishes on classes that are detected by topological modular forms.

The Goodwillie Tower of the Identity Functor on Spaces

Goodwillie calculus approximates a homotopy functor F : Spaces → Spaces by a tower of polynomial functors PnF whose layers DnF = hofib(PnF → Pn−1F) are homogeneous of degree n. For the identity functor Id, the tower converges for simply connected spaces, and the layers are given by DnId(X) ≃ (∂nId ∧ X∧n)hΣn, where ∂nId is the n‑th derivative of the identity, a spectrum with Σn‑action. Arone and Mahowald identified ∂nId as the Spanier‑Whitehead dual of the partition complex of an n‑element set, which is homotopy equivalent to a wedge of spheres of dimension n−2. Consequently, the layers of the Goodwillie tower of the identity are built from the homology of the partition lattice, and the tower encodes the stable homotopy groups of spheres via the spectral sequence associated to the tower.

Derivatives and the partition complex

The n‑th derivative ∂nId is the spectrum Σ∞(|Πn|+)∨, where Πn is the poset of proper non‑trivial partitions of an n‑element set. The homotopy type of |Πn| is a wedge of (n−2)‑spheres, one for each maximal chain in the partition lattice. The Σn‑action permutes these spheres according to the action on partitions. This description makes the layers DnId(X) computable in terms of the homology of the symmetric groups with coefficients in the partition complex. For n = 2, ∂2Id ≃ S0 with the sign representation, giving D2Id(X) ≃ Σ−1(X ∧ X)hΣ₂, which is the quadratic approximation to the identity.

Convergence and the stable homotopy groups

The Goodwillie tower of the identity converges for any simply connected space X, meaning the natural map X → holim PnId(X) is a weak equivalence. For non‑simply connected spaces the tower need not converge; the obstruction is measured by the fundamental group. The associated spectral sequence has E₁‑page π*DnId(X) and abuts to π*X. When X = S0, the spectral sequence computes the stable homotopy groups of spheres. The E₁‑term is the homology of the symmetric groups with coefficients in the partition complex, which is known to be related to the Lie operad and to the homology of the moduli space of curves. This provides a conceptual bridge between the calculus of functors and the algebraic geometry of Mg,n.

Higher Topos Theory and Sheaf Cohomology in Algebraic Topology

Higher topos theory, developed by Lurie, provides a framework for doing sheaf theory on ∞‑categories. An ∞‑topos is an ∞‑category of sheaves of spaces on an ∞‑site, satisfying descent. In algebraic topology, the ∞‑topos of sheaves on the étale site of a scheme or on the site of manifolds with the open cover topology allows one to define cohomology with coefficients in any sheaf of spectra. The global sections functor Γ : Shv(C, Sp) → Sp has a left adjoint, the constant sheaf functor, and the derived functors of Γ compute sheaf cohomology. For a topological space X, the ∞‑topos of sheaves on X recovers classical singular cohomology when the coefficient sheaf is the Eilenberg‑MacLane spectrum HZ. For a scheme S, the étale ∞‑topos with coefficients in the p‑adic Tate twist gives étale cohomology, which by the work of Scholze and others is related to the p‑adic completion of algebraic K‑theory via the cyclotomic trace.

Descent and hypercompleteness

An ∞‑topos is hypercomplete if every ∞‑connected morphism is an equivalence. The ∞‑topos of sheaves on a Noetherian scheme of finite Krull dimension is hypercomplete, but the ∞‑topos of sheaves on a general topological space need not be. Hypercompleteness ensures that Čech cohomology agrees with sheaf cohomology. In the context of algebraic topology, the ∞‑topos of parametrized spectra over a base B (the slice ∞‑topos Sp/B) is hypercomplete, and its global sections compute the cohomology of B with local coefficients. This perspective underlies the six‑functor formalism for parametrized spectra developed by May‑Sigurdsson and by Lurie.

Applications to chromatic homotopy theory

The moduli stack of formal groups Mfg carries a sheaf of E∞‑ring spectra Otop constructed by Goerss‑Hopkins‑Miller. The global sections of this sheaf over the compactified moduli M̄ell give the spectrum TMF. Higher topos theory provides the language to state and prove descent for Otop: the map TMF → Γ(M̄ell, Otop) is an equivalence because the cover by affine charts is a hypercover in the étale ∞‑topos. This descent property is essential for the computation of the homotopy groups of TMF via the Adams‑Novikov spectral sequence, where the E₂‑page is the sheaf cohomology of M̄ell with coefficients in the sheaf of invariant differentials on the universal elliptic curve.

  • Confusing the string orientation with the sigma orientation: the string orientation lands in connective TMF, while the sigma orientation lands in periodic tmf; the latter inverts the discriminant Δ and loses torsion information.
  • Assuming the telescope conjecture holds at all heights and primes: it is known only for n=1 (all p) and n=2 at p=3; at p=2 it is widely expected to fail.
  • Treating the Mahowald invariant as defined on all stable stems: it is only defined on the 2‑primary part and requires the class not to be in the image of h₀.
  • Believing the Goodwillie tower of the identity converges for all spaces: convergence is proven only for simply connected spaces; for non‑simply connected spaces the tower may not recover the original space.
  • Overlooking hypercompleteness when applying descent in an ∞‑topos: Čech cohomology computes sheaf cohomology only in hypercomplete ∞‑topoi; the étale ∞‑topos of a Noetherian scheme is hypercomplete, but the ∞‑topos of sheaves on an arbitrary topological space may not be.
  • Remember to verify each statement against standard references.

Localization and Completion of Spectra

Localization and completion are the two fundamental operations that allow one to isolate the behavior of a spectrum at a chosen set of primes or with respect to a chosen homology theory. For a spectrum X and a homology theory E, the E-localization LEX is the initial E-local spectrum under X; a spectrum Y is E-local if the map [Y, Z] → [Y, LEZ] is an isomorphism for every spectrum Z. Bousfield proved in 1979 that such a localization functor exists for every homology theory and that it is smashing when E is a ring spectrum. The most common cases are localization at a prime p, denoted Lp, and localization with respect to Morava K-theory K(n), denoted LK(n). Completion, on the other hand, is a limit construction: the E-nilpotent completion of X is the homotopy limit of the tower X → LEX → LELEX → …. When E = Hℤ/p this recovers the classical p-completion of Bousfield–Kan. The interplay between localization and completion is governed by the fracture square: for a finite spectrum X there is a pullback diagram X → LpX × LℚX → LpLℚX. This square is the starting point for many chromatic calculations, because it reduces global problems to p-local and rational pieces. A subtle point is that LK(n) does not preserve finite spectra; the K(n)-local category is a genuine stable homotopy category in its own right, with its own Picard group and duality. The work of Devinatz–Hopkins–Smith on the nilpotence and periodicity theorems shows that the K(n)-local categories detect all nilpotent maps between finite spectra, making them the natural home for chromatic homotopy theory.

Operads and ∞-Operads in Stable Homotopy Theory

Operads encode algebraic structures with multiple inputs and one output, and their homotopy theory provides a rigid framework for E∞ ring spectra, A∞ ring spectra, and more exotic structures such as En algebras. The classical Boardman–Vogt W-construction replaces a topological operad by a cofibrant one, allowing the transfer of algebra structures along weak equivalences. In the ∞-categorical setting developed by Lurie, an ∞-operad is a map of ∞-categories O⊗ → Fin* satisfying the Segal condition; algebras over an ∞-operad are sections of this map. The E∞ operad is the nerve of the symmetric groups, and its algebras in spectra are precisely the commutative ring spectra up to coherent homotopy. The En operads interpolate between A∞ (n = 1) and E∞ (n = ∞); their algebras are n-fold loop spaces by the recognition principle of May and Boardman–Vogt. A key computational tool is the factorization homology of En algebras, which for a framed n-manifold M produces a spectrum ∫M A that satisfies excision and is symmetric monoidal in M. The Dunn additivity theorem identifies En+m algebras with En algebras in Em algebras, providing a powerful inductive handle.
