More precisely, if R is a K(n)-local E∞-ring (or more generally, a v_n-periodic ring spectrum), then K(R) should be vn+1-periodic. The conjecture is motivated by the computations: K(ℤ) has chromatic height 1 (detected by K(1)-localization), K(ku) has chromatic height 2 (detected by K(2)-localization), and K(KO) at p=2 has chromatic height 2. The Dundas-Goodwillie-McCarthy theorem provides a rigorous framework: for a nilpotent extension of connective ring spectra R → R', the relative K-theory K(R, R') is equivalent to the relative topological cyclic homology TC(R, R'). Since TC is built from the cyclotomic structure on THH, and THH(R) is a module over THH(S) ≃ S, the chromatic complexity of TC(R) is closely related to that of R. The cyclotomic trace K(R) → TC(R) is a map of E∞-ring spectra. For R = ℤ_p, TC(ℤ_p) is known to be K(1)-local (after p-completion), matching K(ℤ_p). For R = ku_p (p-completed connective complex K-theory), Ausoni and Rognes computed that TC(ku_p) is K(2)-local, and the trace map K(ku_p) → TC(ku_p) is a K(2)-local equivalence in a range. The redshift conjecture remains open in general, but significant progress has been made: Clausen, Mathew, and others have shown that for certain E∞-rings, the K-theory indeed exhibits the predicted chromatic height increase. The conjecture is also related to the telescope conjecture: if the telescope conjecture holds for K(n)-local spectra, then the v_n-periodic part of K(R) can be detected by the telescope of a v_n self-map. The algebraic K-theory of the sphere spectrum K(S) is the algebraic K-theory of the stable homotopy category; its chromatic height is expected to be 1 (since S is K(0)-local), but the full computation of K(S) remains elusive. The redshift philosophy extends to other invariants: topological Hochschild homology THH(R) is expected to have the same chromatic height as R, while TC(R) and K(R) increase height by one. This pattern is a guiding principle in the study of trace methods and the classification of E∞-ring spectra.

  • Confusing the arithmetic fracture square (rational vs p-complete) with the chromatic fracture square (E(n)-local vs K(n+1)-local) — they operate at different levels of localization.
  • Assuming the chromatic tower converges for all spectra — chromatic convergence holds for finite spectra but fails for many infinite spectra (e.g., the sphere spectrum itself at p=2 has a non-trivial lim1 term).
  • Treating E∞-ring spectra as strictly commutative ring objects — the correct setting is ∞-categories where commutativity is coherent up to all higher homotopies; strict models exist but are not unique.
  • Overlooking that the Morava stabilizer group 𝔾_n is a profinite group and its cohomology must be taken in the continuous sense — discrete group cohomology gives the wrong answer.
  • Confusing geometric fixed points Φ^H with categorical fixed points (-)^H in genuine G-spectra — they agree only for trivial representations and differ crucially for representation spheres.
  • Believing the redshift conjecture is a theorem — it is a conjecture supported by computations for ℤ, ku, KO, and some other cases, but not proved in general.
  • Neglecting that the norm functor N^G_H is left adjoint to restriction, not right adjoint — this determines its behavior with respect to colimits and the smash product.
The fracture squares, Morava stabilizer cohomology, genuine equivariant spectra, and the redshift conjecture together form the computational and conceptual backbone of modern chromatic homotopy theory and its applications to geometric topology.

The Chromatic Fracture Square and its Role in Localization

The chromatic fracture square is a fundamental tool for reconstructing a spectrum from its localizations at successive Morava K‑theories. For a fixed prime p and height n, let LK(n) denote Bousfield localization with respect to the Morava K‑theory K(n) and let LE(n) denote localization with respect to the Johnson–Wilson theory E(n). The square

Chromatic fracture square
LK(n)XLE(n)X
LE(n-1)XLE(n-1)LK(n)XLE(n-1)LE(n)X

is a homotopy pullback. The vertical maps are the canonical localizations, and the horizontal maps are induced by the unit maps of the ring spectra K(n) → E(n) and E(n-1) → E(n). The square expresses the fact that the K(n)-local and E(n)-local information together determine the E(n-1)-local information. In practice one uses the square to compute homotopy groups of a spectrum X by first understanding its K(n)-local and E(n)-local approximations, then gluing them along the E(n-1)-local layer. The fracture square is compatible with the Adams–Novikov spectral sequence: the E2-page for LK(n)X is the continuous cohomology of the Morava stabilizer group with coefficients in the K(n)-homology of X, while the E2-page for LE(n)X involves the same group but with discrete coefficients. The passage from discrete to continuous cohomology is precisely the map induced by the vertical arrow in the fracture square. This perspective underlies many modern computations, for example the determination of the K(2)-local sphere at p = 3 by Behrens and others. A common mistake is to treat the fracture square as a product decomposition; it is only a pullback, and the extension data encoded in the homotopy limit can be highly non‑trivial.

The Norm Map and the Hill–Hopkins–Ravenel Norm Theorem in Genuine G‑Spectra

In genuine G‑equivariant stable homotopy theory the norm functor NGH associated to a subgroup inclusion H ≤ G is a symmetric monoidal left adjoint to the restriction functor ResGH. For a genuine H‑spectrum X, the norm NGHX is a genuine G‑spectrum whose geometric fixed points satisfy ΦK(NGHX) ≃ ∧g∈KG/H ΦgHg⁻¹∩K(X) for any subgroup K ≤ G. The Hill–Hopkins–Ravenel norm theorem asserts that for a finite group G and a family ℱ of subgroups, the map

∨(H)∈ℱ NGH (XhH) → X

is an equivalence after K(n)-localization, provided X is a K(n)-local G‑spectrum with suitable completeness hypotheses. This result is the equivariant analogue of the chromatic fracture square and is the key input in the proof of the Kervaire invariant one problem. The norm map also controls the relationship between categorical fixed points (−)H and geometric fixed points ΦH. For a representation sphere SV the geometric fixed points ΦHSV are SVH, while the categorical fixed points (SV)H are the unreduced suspension of the unit sphere in VH. The norm functor reconciles these two constructions via the tom Dieck splitting. In computations, one often replaces a genuine G‑spectrum by its underlying non‑equivariant spectrum with a G‑action, applies the norm to build induced spectra, and then uses the Greenlees–May decomposition to recover the genuine fixed points. A frequent trap is to assume that the norm preserves all colimits; it preserves colimits because it is a left adjoint, but it does not preserve limits, and the interaction with the smash product is governed by the projection formula NGH(X ∧ ResGHY) ≃ NGHX ∧ Y.

Worked Example: Norm of a Trivial H‑Spectrum
Let G = C2 act trivially on a spectrum X. Compute the geometric fixed points ΦC2(NC2eX) and compare with the categorical fixed points (NC2eX)C2.
For the trivial subgroup e ≤ C2, the norm NC2eX is the induced spectrum X ∧ X with the swap action. Its geometric fixed points are ΦC2(X ∧ X) ≃ ΦeX ∧ ΦeX ≃ X ∧ X because the only subgroup of C2 intersecting e trivially is e itself. The categorical fixed points are the homotopy fixed points of the swap action on X ∧ X, which is the homotopy orbit spectrum (X ∧ X)hC2. In general X ∧ X and (X ∧ X)hC2 are not equivalent; they agree only after K(n)-localization for suitable n by the norm theorem. This illustrates the essential difference between geometric and categorical fixed points.
Exam Strategy
When a question asks for the effect of the norm on fixed points, first write the tom Dieck splitting formula for ΦK(NGHX). Identify the double coset decomposition KG/H and the intersections K ∩ gHg⁻¹. Then apply the known behavior of Φ on smash products. For categorical fixed points, recall that (−)K is a right adjoint and commutes with limits, so (NGHX)K ≃ MapG(G/K₊, NGHX). Use the adjunction NGH ⊣ Res to rewrite this as MapH(Res G/K₊, X). This reduces the problem to a computation in H‑spectra.

Continuous Cohomology of the Morava Stabilizer Group and the Goerss–Hopkins Obstruction Theory

The Morava stabilizer group 𝔾n = Aut(Γn) ⋊ Gal(𝔽pⁿ/𝔽p) is a profinite group acting on the Lubin–Tate deformation ring of a height‑n formal group law Γn over 𝔽pⁿ. Its continuous cohomology Hscts(𝔾n; M) with coefficients in a discrete 𝔾n-module M computes the E2-page of the K(n)-local Adams–Novikov spectral sequence for the sphere. The Goerss–Hopkins obstruction theory provides a systematic method to lift a map of Morava modules to a map of E∞-ring spectra. The obstruction groups are precisely the continuous cohomology groups Hs+2cts(𝔾n; πtLK(n)X) for a K(n)-local spectrum X. In particular, the existence of an E∞-structure on a K(n)-local ring spectrum R is obstructed by classes in H3cts(𝔾n; π*R). The theory also yields a classification of E∞-structures up to equivalence: the set of equivalence classes is a torsor under H2cts(𝔾n; π*R). A crucial technical point is that the coefficient modules must be taken with the discrete topology; using the profinite topology on the coefficients would give the wrong groups. The continuous cohomology of 𝔾n is known in low degrees: for n = 1, 𝔾1 ≅ ℤp× and its cohomology is the group cohomology of ℤp×, which is periodic of period 2. For n = 2 and p ≥ 5, the cohomology has been computed by Shimomura and others, revealing a rich pattern of periodic families that correspond to the Greek letter elements in the stable homotopy groups of spheres. The Goerss–Hopkins theory has been applied to construct the K(n)-local E∞-ring spectra representing topological modular forms (TMF) at various primes, and to prove the uniqueness of the Morava E-theory En as an E∞-ring. A common error is to confuse the continuous cohomology of 𝔾n with the discrete group cohomology of its dense subgroup of automorphisms of the formal group; the latter misses the Galois action and yields incorrect obstruction groups.

The Adams–Novikov Spectral Sequence for the Sphere Spectrum at Odd Primes

The Adams–Novikov spectral sequence (ANSS) for the sphere spectrum S0 at an odd prime p has E2-page ExtBP*BPs,t(BP*, BP*) where BP is the Brown–Peterson spectrum. By the Landweber exact functor theorem this Ext group is isomorphic to the continuous cohomology Hscts(𝔾n; (En)t) after K(n)-localization. At odd primes the first few stems have been completely determined: the 0‑stem is ℤ(p), the 1‑stem is 0, the 2‑stem is 0, the 3‑stem is ℤ/p generated by α1, the 4‑stem is 0, the 5‑stem is ℤ/p generated by β1, and the 6‑stem is 0. The element α1 corresponds to the first Greek letter family and survives to the E∞-page; β1 is the first beta element and also survives. The first potential differential is d3(β1) = α1³, but at odd primes this differential vanishes because α1³ = 0 in the E2-page. The next interesting differential is d5(β2) = α1β1², which is non‑zero and kills β2. The pattern of differentials is governed by the chromatic filtration: elements of filtration n are detected by the Morava stabilizer group of height n. The ANSS at p = 3 has been pushed up to the 100‑stem by the work of Ravenel, Shimomura, and others, using the May spectral sequence to compute the Ext groups over the Hopf algebroid (BP*, BP*BP). The May spectral sequence introduces a filtration by the length of monomials in the generators ti of the dual Steenrod algebra, and its E1-page is a polynomial algebra on classes hi,j. The differentials in the May spectral sequence correspond to the relations in the Hopf algebroid and can be read off from the coproduct formulas. A frequent trap is to assume that the ANSS collapses at E2 for the sphere at odd primes; in fact there are many non‑trivial differentials, and the only known general collapse result is for the K(n)-local sphere after a suitable localization.

Goodwillie Derivatives of the Identity Functor on Spectra and the Partition Complex

Goodwillie calculus assigns to a homotopy functor F from spectra to spectra a tower of polynomial approximations PnF → Pn-1F. The layers DnF = hofib(PnF → Pn-1F) are homogeneous degree‑n functors, classified by spectra with Σn-action called the n‑th derivative ∂nF. For the identity functor Id on spectra, the derivatives are known: ∂nId ≃ S-n ∧ |Part(n)|, where Part(n) is the partition complex of the set {1,…,n}. The partition complex is the geometric realization of the poset of proper partitions of {1,…,n}, ordered by refinement. It is a wedge of (n-2)! spheres of dimension n-2, and the Σn-action is the Lie representation. Consequently DnId(X) ≃ (X∧n ∧ S-n ∧ |Part(n)|)hΣn. The Goodwillie tower of the identity converges for all connective spectra, and its limit is the identity itself. The convergence fails for non‑connective spectra; for example the tower for the sphere spectrum S0 does not converge to S0 but to its connective cover. The partition complex also appears in the description of the derivatives of the functor Ω∞Σ∞ and in the homology of the mapping class groups via the Madsen–Weiss theorem. A key computational tool is the Arone–Mahowald spectral sequence, which computes the homotopy groups of the layers DnId(S0) in terms of the homology of the partition complex and the stable homotopy groups of spheres. The first few layers give the well‑known splitting Σ∞Ω∞S0 ≃ ∨n≥1 DnId(S0). The derivative ∂2Id is S-2 with the sign representation of Σ2, yielding D2Id(X) ≃ (X ∧ X)hΣ2 ∧ S-2, which is the quadratic construction. A common mistake is to treat the partition complex as a discrete set; its homotopy type is essential for the Σn-action and for the convergence properties of the tower.

  • Confusing the chromatic fracture square (E(n)-local vs K(n+1)-local) with the arithmetic fracture square (rational vs p-complete) — they operate at different localization levels.
  • Assuming the norm functor NGH is a right adjoint; it is left adjoint to restriction, so it preserves colimits but not limits.
  • Treating continuous cohomology of the Morava stabilizer group as discrete group cohomology — the profinite topology is essential for correct obstruction groups.
  • Believing the Adams–Novikov spectral sequence for the sphere collapses at E2 at odd primes — many differentials (e.g., d5(β2)) are non‑zero.
  • Thinking the Goodwillie tower of the identity converges for all spectra — it converges only for connective spectra; for non‑connective spectra it converges to the connective cover.
  • Neglecting that the partition complex |Part(n)| is a wedge of spheres with the Lie representation, not a discrete set — this affects the Σn-action on the derivatives.
  • Overlooking the distinction between geometric fixed points ΦH and categorical fixed points (−)H in genuine G‑spectra — they agree only for trivial representations.
The advanced topics above illustrate how modern algebraic topology intertwines chromatic localization, equivariant norms, profinite group cohomology, spectral sequence computations, and functor calculus to solve deep problems in stable homotopy theory.

The Atiyah-Hirzebruch Spectral Sequence: Generalized Cohomology and Filtration by Skeleta

The Atiyah-Hirzebruch spectral sequence (AHSS) is the primary tool for computing the value of a generalized cohomology theory E^* on a CW complex X. It arises from the skeletal filtration X0 ⊂ X1 ⊂ … ⊂ X and the associated exact couple. The E2-page is given by ordinary cohomology with coefficients in the coefficient groups of the theory: E2p,q = H^p(X; E^q(pt)). The differential d_r has bidegree (r, 1-r) and the sequence converges to the associated graded groups of a filtration on Ep+q(X). When E is complex K-theory KU, the coefficient ring is Z[u, u-1] with |u| = -2, so the E2-page consists of even rows only. The first potentially non-zero differential is d3, which on a class x ∈ H^p(X; Z) is given by d3(x) = operatorname{Sq}3(x) + higher order terms; for KU this differential is identified with the integral Steenrod operation Sq3 composed with the Bockstein. In complex cobordism MU, the coefficient ring is the Lazard ring, and the AHSS collapses at E2 for simply connected spaces of low dimension, but higher differentials encode the formal group law. A key feature is the edge homomorphism Ep,0_∞ → H^p(X; E0(pt)), which coincides with the map induced by the unit of the ring spectrum. The AHSS is natural with respect to maps of spaces and of cohomology theories, making it a powerful computational device for comparing different theories.

Convergence issues are subtle: for a finite CW complex the spectral sequence converges strongly. For infinite complexes one must consider the limit of the filtration; the AHSS converges conditionally if the filtration is exhaustive and Hausdorff. The differentials are often determined by known calculations on spheres and projective spaces. For example, the AHSS for KU^*(CP^n) has E2 = Z[x]/(xn+1) ⊗ Z[u, u-1] with |x| = 2. The differential d3(x^k) = 0 for all k, so the sequence collapses at E2 and yields KU0(CP^n) ≅ Z[x]/(xn+1). In contrast, for real K-theory KO the d3 differential is non-trivial and detects the first Stiefel-Whitney class. The AHSS also underlies the construction of the Adams-Novikov spectral sequence when one replaces ordinary cohomology by MU-cohomology.

The Bockstein Spectral Sequence: Connecting Homology with Different Coefficients

The Bockstein spectral sequence (BSS) originates from a short exact sequence of coefficient groups 0 → A xrightarrow{× p} A → A/p → 0, typically with A = Z and p a prime. Applying homology yields a long exact sequence for each space X; splicing these together produces an exact couple whose derived spectral sequence has E1p,q = Hp+q(X; Z/p) and d1 equal to the Bockstein homomorphism β associated to the coefficient sequence. The E2-page is the homology of the Bockstein complex, which is isomorphic to the p-torsion subgroup of H_*(X; Z) in each degree. Higher differentials d_r correspond to higher Bockstein operations β_r and measure the divisibility of torsion classes by higher powers of p. The BSS converges to the p-primary part of H_*(X; Z) filtered by the exponent of p. In cohomology the dual construction yields a spectral sequence with E1 = H^*(X; Z/p) converging to the p-torsion in H^*(X; Z). The BSS is intimately linked to the Steenrod algebra: the Bockstein β is the Steenrod operation Sq1 at p=2 and the operation β at odd primes. The higher Bocksteins generate the subalgebra of the Steenrod algebra known as the Bockstein algebra. A classic application is the computation of the homology of Eilenberg-Mac Lane spaces K(Z/p, n); the BSS collapses at E2 for these spaces, giving a clean description of their p-torsion. The BSS also appears in the Adams spectral sequence as the E1-page of the May spectral sequence when one filters the cobar complex by the length of monomials in the dual Steenrod algebra generators.

Worked Example: First Non‑trivial Differential in the AHSS for KU^*(CP^∞)
Compute the differential d3 on the generator x ∈ H2(CP^∞; Z) in the Atiyah-Hirzebruch spectral sequence for complex K-theory.
The E2-page is H^*(CP^∞; Z[u, u-1]) ≅ Z[x] ⊗ Z[u, u-1] with |x| = 2, |u| = -2. The AHSS for a complex-oriented theory has d3(x) = c1(L) · β(u) where L is the tautological line bundle and β is the Bott periodicity isomorphism. In KU the first Chern class of L is x and β(u) = 1. The integral Steenrod operation Sq3 vanishes on x because x has degree 2. However, the differential d3 in the AHSS for KU is known to be zero on all classes for CP^∞; the sequence collapses at E2. This can be seen by naturality from the collapse for CP^n (finite skeleta) and the fact that the filtration is Hausdorff. Hence d3(x) = 0. The resulting E_∞-page gives KU0(CP^∞) ≅ Z[[x]] completed at the augmentation ideal.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a spectral sequence question appears, first write down the E2-page explicitly using the coefficient ring and the ordinary (co)homology of the space. Identify the filtration degree and the bidegree of each differential. Use known collapses for projective spaces, spheres, and Lie groups as anchors. If a differential is not forced by degree reasons, check naturality with respect to a map to a space where the calculation is known. Remember that the AHSS for a ring cohomology theory is a spectral sequence of algebras, so differentials satisfy the Leibniz rule.

The Adams Spectral Sequence for Modules over Ring Spectra: Ext over the Steenrod Algebra and its Generalizations

The classical Adams spectral sequence computes the stable homotopy groups of spheres by taking a suitable resolution of the sphere spectrum by spectra whose homotopy groups are known. For a general ring spectrum R and an R-module M, one constructs an Adams resolution using the cobar complex on the Hopf algebroid (R_*, R_*R). The E2-term is operatorname{Ext}R_*Rs,t(R_*, M_*), where the Ext groups are taken in the category of comodules over the Hopf algebroid. When R = HF_p, the Hopf algebroid is the dual Steenrod algebra A_*, and the E2-page becomes operatorname{Ext}A_*s,t(F_p, H_*(M; F_p)). This recovers the classical Adams spectral sequence for the p-completion of M. For R = MU, the Hopf algebroid is (MU_*, MU_*MU) and the resulting spectral sequence is the Adams-Novikov spectral sequence. The change-of-rings theorem identifies operatorname{Ext}MU_*MU(MU_*, MU_*(X)) with operatorname{Ext}BP_*BP(BP_*, BP_*(X)) after p-localization, where BP is the Brown-Peterson spectrum. The May spectral sequence provides a practical method for computing these Ext groups by filtering the cobar complex by the length of monomials in the generators t_i of the dual Steenrod algebra. Its E1-page is a polynomial algebra on classes hi,j, and the differentials encode the coproduct formulas. Convergence of the Adams spectral sequence for a module M holds when M is connective and the resolution is built from connective spectra; the limit is the p-completion of the homotopy groups of M. A frequent subtlety is that the Adams spectral sequence for a non‑connective spectrum (e.g., the sphere spectrum S0) does not converge to the homotopy groups of the spectrum but to those of its connective cover.

The Mahowald Root Invariant and the Detection of Periodic Families

The root invariant, introduced by Mark Mahowald, is a homomorphism R: π_*^S → π_*^S that detects v_n-periodic families in the stable homotopy groups of spheres. It is constructed using the Adams spectral sequence: given an element x ∈ π_k^S represented by a permanent cycle in the Adams E_∞-page, one considers the minimal Adams filtration in which x appears and then follows the effect of multiplying by the periodicity element v_n in the appropriate Morava K-theory. The root invariant of the Hopf invariant one elements α1 ∈ π1^S, α2 ∈ π3^S, α3 ∈ π7^S yields the first Greek letter elements β1, β2, β3 in the image of J. For the alpha family αp-1 ∈ π2p-3^S at odd primes, the root invariant maps αp-1 to the beta element β1 in the v1-periodic family. The root invariant respects the chromatic filtration: if an element has chromatic height n, its root invariant has height at least n. Mahowald's computation of the root invariant for the first few stems revealed that the v1-periodic family generated by β1 is the root invariant of the image of J. The root invariant also interacts with the Kahn-Priddy transfer and the Segal conjecture: the transfer map in stable homotopy induces a map on root invariants that corresponds to the inclusion of the v1-periodic part. A key technical tool is the Mahowald spectral sequence, which computes the root invariant by filtering the Adams resolution by the chromatic tower. The root invariant of the Kervaire invariant elements θ_j (when they exist) is known to be zero, reflecting their non‑periodic nature. Understanding the root invariant is essential for organizing the stable stems into chromatic layers and for predicting the existence of higher Greek letter elements.

The Chromatic Fracture Square and the Arithmetic Fracture Square

Two fundamental pullback squares govern the decomposition of a spectrum into its chromatic and arithmetic pieces. The chromatic fracture square for a p-local spectrum X and an integer n ≥ 0 is a homotopy pullback diagram begin{array}{ccc} LE(n)X & longrightarrow & LK(n)X \\ downarrow & & downarrow \\ LE(n-1)X & longrightarrow & LK(n)LE(n-1)X end{array} where E(n) is the Johnson-Wilson spectrum and K(n) is the Morava K-theory of height n. This square expresses the E(n)-localization of X in terms of its K(n)-localization and its E(n-1)-localization. Iterating yields the chromatic tower converging to the p-completion of X. The arithmetic fracture square, on the other hand, relates the rationalization and the p-completion of a spectrum: begin{array}{ccc} X & longrightarrow & XQ \\ downarrow & & downarrow \\ X_p^wedge & longrightarrow & (X_p^wedge)Q end{array} This is a homotopy pullback for any spectrum X. The two squares operate at different localization levels: the chromatic fracture square lives in the p-local stable homotopy category and involves Bousfield localizations with respect to E(n) and K(n); the arithmetic fracture square is a statement about the interplay between rational and p-adic information. In the construction of topological modular forms operatorname{tmf}, both squares are used: the arithmetic fracture square glues the p-complete and rational pieces of the compactified moduli stack of elliptic curves, while the chromatic fracture square analyzes the K(2)-local and K(1)-local layers of the resulting spectrum. A common mistake is to confuse the two squares or to assume that the chromatic fracture square is a pullback in the category of all spectra without first localizing at p. The correct statement requires p-localization, and the maps involved are the natural transformations between the respective localization functors.

  • Assuming the Atiyah-Hirzebruch spectral sequence always collapses at E2 for complex-oriented theories; higher differentials can be non-zero (e.g., d3 in KO-theory).
  • Treating the Bockstein spectral sequence as a first-quadrant spectral sequence without checking the convergence conditions for infinite complexes.
  • Confusing the Adams spectral sequence for a ring spectrum with the classical Adams spectral sequence; the Hopf algebroid changes from the dual Steenrod algebra to (R_*, R_*R).
  • Believing the root invariant is defined on all stable stems without reference to the Adams filtration; it is only defined for elements that survive to the E_∞-page of the Adams spectral sequence.
  • Mixing the chromatic fracture square (which involves E(n) and K(n) localizations) with the arithmetic fracture square (which involves rationalization and p-completion); they are distinct pullbacks in different localized categories.
  • Neglecting that the May spectral sequence computes operatorname{Ext} over the dual Steenrod algebra, not over the Steenrod algebra itself; the generators t_i are dual to the Milnor basis.
  • Overlooking the requirement that the Adams resolution be built from connective spectra for strong convergence; for non-connective spectra the limit is the connective cover.

These advanced topics illustrate how spectral sequences, chromatic localization, and functor calculus intertwine to reveal the deep structure of stable homotopy theory.

The Dyer–Lashof Algebra and Homology of Infinite Loop Spaces

The Dyer–Lashof algebra R (at an odd prime p) and its mod 2 analogue govern the homology operations on infinite loop spaces. For a connected infinite loop space X = Ω∞Y, the homology H*(X; 𝔽p) carries a natural action of R that commutes with the Pontryagin product and the coproduct. The algebra is generated by operations Qs (degree s) at p = 2 and by βεQs (ε ∈ {0,1}) at odd primes, subject to Adem relations and Cartan formulas. The Nishida relations describe how these operations interact with the Steenrod algebra action on the homology of the spectrum Y. A fundamental result is the recognition principle: a connected space X is an infinite loop space if and only if its homology is an algebra over the Dyer–Lashof algebra satisfying certain compatibility conditions. The homology of the free infinite loop space on a sphere, Ω∞Σ∞Sn, is the free R-algebra on a generator in degree n. This structure underpins the computation of H*(Ω∞Σ∞S0; 𝔽p) and the identification of the image of the J-homomorphism via the Kahn–Priddy theorem. The Dyer–Lashof operations also control the homology of the extended power constructions Dp(X) = (EΣp ×Σp X∧p)hΣp, which are central to the construction of the E∞ operad and the proof of the nilpotence theorem.

The Chromatic Fracture Square and the Arithmetic Fracture Square

Two homotopy pullback squares govern the decomposition of a spectrum into its chromatic and arithmetic pieces. The chromatic fracture square for a p-local spectrum X and integer n ≥ 0 is
LE(n)X → LK(n)X
↓           ↓
LE(n−1)X → LK(n)LE(n−1)X
where E(n) is the Johnson–Wilson spectrum (with E(0) = Hℚ) and K(n) is the Morava K-theory of height n. This square expresses the E(n)-localization in terms of the K(n)-localization and the E(n−1)-localization. Iterating yields the chromatic tower X → ⋯ → LE(n)X → LE(n−1)X → ⋯ → LE(0)X = Xℚ, whose limit is the p-completion Xp∧. The arithmetic fracture square relates rationalization and p-completion for any spectrum X:
X → Xℚ
↓           ↓
Xp∧ → (Xp∧)ℚ
This is a homotopy pullback in the category of all spectra. The two squares operate at different levels: the chromatic fracture square lives in the p-local stable homotopy category and involves Bousfield localizations with respect to E(n) and K(n); the arithmetic fracture square is a statement about the interplay between rational and p-adic information. In the construction of topological modular forms tmf, both squares are used: the arithmetic fracture square glues the p-complete and rational pieces of the compactified moduli stack of elliptic curves M̄ell, while the chromatic fracture square analyzes the K(2)-local and K(1)-local layers of the resulting spectrum. A common mistake is to confuse the two squares or to assume the chromatic fracture square is a pullback in the category of all spectra without first localizing at p.

Worked Example: Reconstructing a Spectrum from its Fracture Squares
Let X be a p-local spectrum such that LK(1)X ≃ * and LK(2)X ≃ * but LE(2)X ≢ *. Explain why this situation cannot occur, using the chromatic fracture square for n = 2.
The chromatic fracture square for n = 2 gives a homotopy pullback LE(2)X → LK(2)X with vertical map to LE(1)X. If LK(2)X ≃ *, the pullback forces LE(2)X to be the homotopy fiber of LE(1)X → LK(2)LE(1)X. But LK(2)LE(1)X ≃ * because E(1) is K(2)-acyclic (its formal group has height ≤ 1). Thus the map LE(1)X → * is trivial, so the fiber is LE(1)X. Hence LE(2)X ≃ LE(1)X. By induction, if all K(n)-localizations vanish, the chromatic tower collapses to Xℚ, so X is rational. Therefore a p-local spectrum with trivial K(1) and K(2) localizations but nontrivial E(2)-localization cannot exist.
Exam Strategy: Distinguishing the Two Fracture Squares
When a question mentions a pullback involving E(n) and K(n), it is the chromatic fracture square — always p-local, always requires p-localization first. When a question mentions a pullback involving Xℚ and Xp∧, it is the arithmetic fracture square — works for any spectrum, no p-localization needed. Write the diagrams side by side on scrap paper; the vertical maps in the chromatic square are localizations, while in the arithmetic square they are completion and rationalization. The chromatic square iterates to give the chromatic tower; the arithmetic square does not iterate.

The Root Invariant and the Mahowald Spectral Sequence

The root invariant R : π*S → π*S is a secondary operation defined on elements that survive to the E∞-page of the Adams spectral sequence. Given a permanent cycle x ∈ Exts,tA(𝔽p, 𝔽p) representing an element in the s-stem, its root invariant is constructed by lifting x to the E1-page of the Adams resolution, applying the connecting homomorphism of the cofiber sequence defining the resolution, and projecting back to the E∞-page. The result is an element R(x) in a higher stem, well-defined modulo indeterminacy. For the alpha family αp−1 ∈ π2p−3S at odd primes, R(αp−1) = β1, the first beta element in the v1-periodic family. The root invariant respects the chromatic filtration: if an element has chromatic height n, its root invariant has height at least n. Mahowald's computation of the root invariant for the first few stems revealed that the v1-periodic family generated by β1 is the root invariant of the image of J. The root invariant also interacts with the Kahn–Priddy transfer and the Segal conjecture: the transfer map in stable homotopy induces a map on root invariants corresponding to the inclusion of the v1-periodic part. The root invariant of the Kervaire invariant elements θj (when they exist) is zero, reflecting their non-periodic nature. A key technical tool is the Mahowald spectral sequence, which computes the root invariant by filtering the Adams resolution by the chromatic tower. Its E1-page is a direct sum of Ext groups over the Morava stabilizer algebras, and its differentials encode the interaction between chromatic layers. Understanding the root invariant is essential for organizing the stable stems into chromatic layers and for predicting the existence of higher Greek letter elements.

The Bott Periodicity Theorem and the Atiyah–Bott–Shapiro Orientation

Bott periodicity is the foundational 8-fold periodicity of real K-theory KO and 2-fold periodicity of complex K-theory KU. For real K-theory, the theorem states that the loop space Ω8BO is homotopy equivalent to BO × ℤ, inducing an isomorphism KOn(X) ≅ KOn+8(X) for all spaces X. For complex K-theory, Ω2BU ≃ BU × ℤ gives KUn(X) ≅ KUn+2(X). The Atiyah–Bott–Shapiro orientation constructs a map of ring spectra MSpin → KO (and MSpinc → KU) using the Clifford module construction. For a spin vector bundle V → X, the associated Clifford algebra bundle Cl(V) has a canonical irreducible module (the spinor bundle) whose K-theory class defines the orientation. This orientation refines the classical Thom isomorphism: for a spin manifold M, the KO-theory fundamental class [M]KO ∈ KOn(M) maps to the KO-theory Thom class of the stable normal bundle. The ABS orientation is the geometric input for the KO-theoretic index theorem: the analytic index of the Dirac operator on a spin manifold equals the topological index given by the Â-genus. In chromatic homotopy theory, the KO-theory spectrum is E(1) at p = 2 (after completion), and the ABS orientation factors through the K(1)-local sphere. The KO-theory Adams–Novikov E2-term is Ext over the Hopf algebroid (KO*, KO*KO), which is computable via the Adams spectral sequence for KO. The KO-theory Adams spectral sequence has E2-page ExtA(1)(𝔽2, 𝔽2) where A(1) is the subalgebra of the Steenrod algebra generated by Sq1 and Sq2, and it converges to the 2-complete stable stems with a v1-periodic pattern detected by the element v1 ∈ π2S.

The Brown–Peterson Spectrum and the Adams–Novikov E2 Term

The Brown–Peterson spectrum BP at a prime p is a commutative ring spectrum with homotopy groups BP* = ℤ(p)[v1, v2, …] where |vi| = 2(pi − 1). It is a summand of the complex bordism spectrum MU(p) and carries the universal p-typical formal group law. The Adams–Novikov spectral sequence (ANSS) for a spectrum X has E2-page ExtBP*BP(BP*, BP*X) and converges to the p-completion of π*(X) under suitable connectivity hypotheses. The Hopf algebroid (BP*, BP*BP) classifies p-typical formal group laws and strict isomorphisms between them. Its cohomology is the ANSS E2 term for the sphere. The chromatic filtration on this Ext group is induced by the filtration of the cobar complex by the height of the formal group: the vn-periodic elements correspond to height n phenomena. The E2 term for the sphere is known to be concentrated in a wedge of vn-periodic families (the Greek letter elements α, β, γ, …) and a v1-periodic part coming from the image of J. The Morava change-of-rings theorem identifies the K(n)-local ANSS E2 term with the continuous cohomology of the Morava stabilizer group Sn with coefficients in the Morava module En*. The chromatic spectral sequence is a spectral sequence of spectral sequences that computes the ANSS E2 term by filtering by chromatic height; its E1 page is a direct sum of cohomology groups of the stabilizer groups. The periodicity theorem (Hopkins–Smith, Devinatz–Hopkins–Smith) states that every vn-periodic element in the ANSS is detected by a vn-self-map of a finite spectrum of type n, and the nilpotence theorem states that any map between finite spectra that is zero in MU-homology is nilpotent. These theorems underpin the entire chromatic program.

  • Confusing the chromatic fracture square (involves E(n) and K(n) localizations, requires p-localization) with the arithmetic fracture square (involves rationalization and p-completion, works globally).
  • Assuming the root invariant is defined on all stable stems; it is only defined for elements that survive to the E∞-page of the Adams spectral sequence.
  • Treating the Dyer–Lashof operations as acting on the homology of any loop space; they require an infinite loop space structure (an E∞ action).
  • Believing Bott periodicity for KO is 2-fold like KU; it is 8-fold, and the ABS orientation MSpin → KO is essential for the KO-theoretic index theorem.
  • Overlooking that the Adams–Novikov E2 term for the sphere is computed via the chromatic spectral sequence, not directly from the cobar complex on BP*BP.
  • Neglecting that the Mahowald spectral sequence computes the root invariant by filtering the Adams resolution by the chromatic tower, not by the Adams filtration.
  • Assuming the arithmetic fracture square implies X ≃ Xℚ × Xp∧; it is a homotopy pullback, not a product, and the maps to the rationalization of the completion are essential.

The Adams Spectral Sequence for Connective K-Theory

The Adams spectral sequence based on connective complex K-theory ku and connective real K-theory ko provides a computational bridge between the classical Adams spectral sequence (based on H𝔽p) and the Adams–Novikov spectral sequence (based on BP). The ku-based Adams spectral sequence has E2-page Extku*ku(ku*, ku*X) converging to the 2-completion of π*(X) for connective spectra X. The Hopf algebroid (ku*, ku*ku) is a quotient of (MU*, MU*MU) by the ideal generated by the Conner–Floyd classes, and its cobar complex is substantially smaller than that of BP. The ku-Adams spectral sequence detects the image of the J-homomorphism completely: the v1-periodic families in the stable stems correspond to the ku-homology of the sphere, and the differentials are governed by the action of the Adams operations ψk. For ko, the 8-fold Bott periodicity induces a spectral sequence with E2-page Extko*ko(ko*, ko*X) converging to the 2-complete stable stems. The ko-Adams spectral sequence is particularly effective for computing the 2-primary stable stems in the range where the image of J dominates, and it provides a natural home for the Mahowald invariant. The ku and ko Adams spectral sequences are related by the complexification and realification maps, and the fiber sequence ko → ku → Σ4ko induces a long exact sequence of spectral sequences. The ku-Adams E2-term for the sphere is concentrated in even degrees and is a polynomial algebra on classes v1k in degree 2k(p−1) at odd primes, while at p=2 it involves the subalgebra A(1) of the Steenrod algebra. The differentials in the ku-Adams spectral sequence for the sphere are known to be generated by d3(v14) = h03v12 at p=2, and the spectral sequence collapses at E4 for the image of J. The ko-Adams spectral sequence has a more complicated E2-term involving the ko-homology of the sphere, which is a module over ko* = ℤ(2)[η, α, β±1] with |η|=1, |α|=4, |β|=8. The ko-Adams differentials detect the 2-primary torsion in the image of J and the first few exotic elements such as η, ν, σ.

The Picard Group of the Global Stable Homotopy Category

The Picard group Pic(𝒮) of the stable homotopy category 𝒮 consists of isomorphism classes of invertible spectra under the smash product, with group operation induced by ∧. An invertible spectrum X satisfies X ∧ Y ≃ 𝕊 for some Y, and such spectra are precisely the finite spectra of type 0 (i.e., with non-zero rational homology) that are dualizable with dual X∨ = F(X, 𝕊). The Picard group fits into a short exact sequence 0 → ℤ → Pic(𝒮) → Pictors(𝒮) → 0, where the ℤ factor is generated by the sphere spectrum 𝕊 and the suspension Σ, and Pictors(𝒮) is the torsion subgroup. The torsion subgroup is detected by the K(n)-local Picard groups: the chromatic fracture square induces a map Pic(𝒮) → ∏n Pic(𝒮K(n)) × Pic(𝒮ℚ), which is injective. The rational Picard group Pic(𝒮ℚ) ≅ ℤ is generated by the rational sphere 𝕊ℚ. The K(n)-local Picard group Pic(𝒮K(n)) was computed by Hopkins–Mahowald and others: it fits into an exact sequence 0 → ℤp → Pic(𝒮K(n)) → ℤ/(pn−1) → 0, where the ℤp factor corresponds to the K(n)-local sphere and its suspensions, and the finite cyclic factor corresponds to the exotic invertible K(n)-local spectra (the "exotic Picard elements"). The global Picard group Pic(𝒮) is not simply the product of the local Picard groups; the chromatic fracture square imposes compatibility conditions. The exotic elements in Pic(𝒮K(n)) do not always lift to global invertible spectra. The only known global exotic invertible spectra are the sphere spectrum and its suspensions; it is a long-standing conjecture that Pic(𝒮) ≅ ℤ, i.e., there are no exotic global invertible spectra. This is known as the "global Picard conjecture." The K(1)-local exotic Picard elements at p=2 are related to the Adams operations on KO and the image of J. The K(2)-local exotic Picard elements at p=3 are related to the tmf spectrum and the Witten genus. The Picard group of the E(n)-local category is larger and includes the Morava stabilizer group action. The computation of Pic(𝒮) is closely tied to the classification of finite spectra of type 0 and the thick subcategory theorem.

Worked Example: Detecting Exotic Picard Elements via the Chromatic Fracture Square
Show that an exotic element in Pic(𝒮K(1)) at p=2 does not lift to a global invertible spectrum by analyzing its image under the chromatic fracture square maps to Pic(𝒮ℚ) and Pic(𝒮K(2)).
An exotic element in Pic(𝒮K(1)) at p=2 corresponds to a non-trivial element in the finite cyclic quotient ℤ/(21−1) = ℤ/1 = 0, so there are no exotic K(1)-local invertible spectra at p=2. At odd primes p, the exotic K(1)-local Picard group is ℤ/(p−1). Take p=3: an exotic element x ∈ Pic(𝒮K(1)) of order 2. The chromatic fracture square for the sphere gives a pullback diagram of Picard groups. The map Pic(𝒮) → Pic(𝒮K(1)) × Pic(𝒮K(2)) × Pic(𝒮ℚ) is injective. The rational Picard group is ℤ, generated by the rational sphere. The K(2)-local Picard group at p=3 has exotic part ℤ/(32−1) = ℤ/8. For x to lift globally, there must exist y ∈ Pic(𝒮K(2)) and n ∈ ℤ such that (x, y, n) satisfies the compatibility conditions in the fracture square. The compatibility condition involves the maps LK(1)LK(2)𝕊 → LK(1)𝕊 and LK(2)LK(1)𝕊 → LK(2)𝕊. The exotic K(1)-local element x has non-trivial image in the K(1)-localization of the K(2)-local sphere, but the K(2)-localization of the K(1)-local sphere is contractible. This forces the K(2)-local component y to be trivial, and the rational component n to be 0. But then the element would be trivial in Pic(𝒮K(1)), contradiction. Hence x does not lift to a global invertible spectrum.
Exam Strategy: Picard Group Computations
When asked about the Picard group of a local or global stable homotopy category, first identify the chromatic height. For K(n)-local categories, recall the exact sequence 0 → ℤ_p → Pic → ℤ/(p^n−1) → 0. For the global category, use the chromatic fracture square: the global Picard group injects into the product of local Picard groups. The key obstruction to lifting exotic local elements is the compatibility of K(n)-localizations of K(m)-local spheres. Remember that L_K(n)L_K(m)𝕊 is contractible for n > m, which kills most lifts. The global Picard conjecture (Pic(𝒮) ≅ ℤ) is still open but known in many cases; cite the Hopkins–Mahowald computation for K(n)-local and the fracture square argument for global.

The Smashing Conjecture and the Classification of Smashing Localizations

The smashing conjecture, formulated by Ravenel, states that every smashing localization functor on the stable homotopy category 𝒮 is equivalent to localization with respect to a homology theory, i.e., it is a Bousfield localization LE for some spectrum E. A localization functor L : 𝒮 → 𝒮 is smashing if the natural map L𝕊 ∧ X → LX is an equivalence for all X. The Bousfield lattice of 𝒮 classifies all Bousfield classes ⟨E⟩, and the smashing localizations correspond to those Bousfield classes that are idempotent (⟨E⟩ ∧ ⟨E⟩ = ⟨E⟩) and satisfy the smashing property. The telescope conjecture, which is a special case of the smashing conjecture for finite localizations, asserts that every finite localization (localization with respect to a finite spectrum) is smashing. The smashing conjecture is known to be false in general: there exist non-smashing localizations, such as the localization with respect to the Brown–Comenetz dual of the sphere I𝕊. However, the classification of smashing localizations is a major open problem. The known smashing localizations include: (1) Localization with respect to a generalized Moore spectrum M(p, v1, …, vn), which are the finite localizations Lnf. (2) Localization with respect to K(n), the K(n)-localization. (3) Localization with respect to E(n), the E(n)-localization. (4) The harmonic localization Lharmonic = L⋁n K(n). (5) The chromatic localization Lchromatic = L⋁n E(n). The smashing conjecture for finite localizations is equivalent to the telescope conjecture. The Bousfield lattice of the stable homotopy category is known to be a proper class, but the sublattice of smashing localizations is a set. The classification of smashing localizations in the K(n)-local category is known: by a theorem of Hovey–Strickland, every smashing localization in the K(n)-local category is a Bousfield localization with respect to a K(n)-local spectrum, and they are classified by the closed subsets of the Balmer spectrum of the K(n)-local category, which is homeomorphic to the set of prime ideals in the Morava stabilizer group. In the global category, the smashing localizations are not fully classified. The work of Barthel–Heard–Valenzuela and others has shown that there are many more smashing localizations than previously thought, including those arising from the "chromatic fracture" of the sphere. The smashing conjecture is closely related to the generating hypothesis: if the generating hypothesis holds (the map from stable homotopy groups to homology is injective), then every localization is smashing. The Freyd generating conjecture is false, but the generating hypothesis for finite spectra is open.

The Anderson Dual of the Sphere Spectrum and Brown–Comenetz Duality

The Anderson dual of the sphere spectrum, denoted I𝕊, is the spectrum representing the functor X ↦ Homℤ(π−*−1(X), ℚ/ℤ) on the stable homotopy category. It is the fiber of the map 𝕊 → 𝕊ℚ in the category of spectra, shifted by one: I𝕊 ≃ Σ−1fib(𝕊 → 𝕊ℚ). The Anderson dual is closely related to Brown–Comenetz duality: for a finite spectrum X, the Brown–Comenetz dual Iℚ/ℤX = F(X, Iℚ/ℤ) satisfies π*(Iℚ/ℤX) ≅ Homℤ(π−*(X), ℚ/ℤ). The Anderson dual I𝕊 is the Brown–Comenetz dual of the sphere: I𝕊 ≃ Iℚ/ℤ𝕊. The homotopy groups of I𝕊 are given by π−1(I𝕊) ≅ ℚ/ℤ, π0(I𝕊) ≅ ℤ̂ (the profinite completion of ℤ), and for k > 0, π−k(I𝕊) ≅ Homℤ(πk−1(𝕊), ℚ/ℤ). The Anderson dual is not a smashing localization: the functor X ↦ F(X, I𝕊) does not commute with smash products. The Anderson self-duality of certain spectra is a powerful tool: a spectrum E is Anderson self-dual if E ≃ ΣdI𝕊 ∧ E for some integer d. The spectrum tmf is Anderson self-dual with a shift of 21 at the prime 2 (after suitable completion), and KO is Anderson self-dual with shift 4. The Anderson dual of the K(n)-local sphere is related to the Gross–Hopkins dual: IK(n)𝕊K(n) ≃ Σ−n2En ∧ I𝕊, where En is the Morava E-theory spectrum. The Brown–Comenetz dual of a finite spectrum X is equivalent to the Anderson dual of its Spanier–Whitehead dual: Iℚ/ℤX ≃ I𝕊 ∧ DX. The Anderson dual plays a crucial role in the formulation of the Adams spectral sequence for the sphere with coefficients in ℚ/ℤ, and in the study of the homotopy groups of spheres via the Anderson exact sequence 0 → Ext(π*−1(𝕊), ℚ/ℤ) → π*(I𝕊) → Hom(π*(𝕊), ℚ/ℤ) → 0. The Anderson dual is also central to the formulation of the "Anderson duality" in the stable homotopy category, which is a contravariant auto-equivalence on the subcategory of finite spectra.

The Homotopy Groups of tmf and the Adams Spectral Sequence for Topological Modular Forms

The spectrum tmf (topological modular forms) is a connective ring spectrum whose homotopy groups π*(tmf) are the global sections of a sheaf of E∞ ring spectra over the compactified moduli stack of elliptic curves M̄ell. The homotopy groups of tmf are computed via the descent spectral sequence (a form of the Adams–Novikov spectral sequence) with E2-page Hs(M̄ell, ωt) converging to π2t−s(tmf), where ω is the line bundle of invariant differentials on the universal elliptic curve. The cohomology groups Hs(M̄ell, ωt) are the ring of modular forms of weight t and level 1, with H0 = MF*(SL2(ℤ)) = ℤ[c4, c6, Δ−1]/(c43 − c62 = 1728Δ) and H1 given by Serre duality. The descent spectral sequence has a horizontal vanishing line at s = 2, and the differentials are determined by the action of the Hecke operators and the geometry of the moduli stack. The Adams spectral sequence for tmf at the prime 2 has E2-page ExtA(2)(𝔽2, 𝔽2) where A(2) is the subalgebra of the Steenrod algebra generated by Sq1, Sq2, Sq4. This spectral sequence converges to the 2-completion of π*(tmf). The tmf Adams spectral sequence is a module over the classical Adams spectral sequence for the sphere, and the map 𝕊 → tmf induces a map of spectral sequences. The homotopy groups of tmf in low degrees are known: π0(tmf) = ℤ, π−1 = 0, π−2 = 0, π1 = 0, π2 = 0, π3 = ℤ/24 (generated by the image of ν), π4 = 0, π5 = 0, π6 = ℤ/2 (generated by c4), π7 = ℤ/2 (generated by c6), π8 = ℤ ⊕ ℤ/2 (generated by c42 and ηc4), and so on. The periodicity in tmf is not Bott periodicity but is governed by the modular form Δ of weight 12, which gives a periodicity element in π−24(tmf) after inverting 6. The string orientation MString → tmf induces the Witten genus on homotopy groups, and the Adams spectral sequence for tmf detects the image of the string bordism groups. The tmf Adams spectral sequence at the prime 3 has E2-page ExtA(1)(𝔽3, 𝔽3) where A(1) is generated by P1 and β, and it converges to the 3-completion of π*(tmf). The computation of π*(tmf) is complete through a range of several hundred degrees using a combination of the descent spectral sequence, the Adams spectral sequence, and the homotopy fixed point spectral sequence for the action of the Morava stabilizer group on E2.

  • Confusing the ku-Adams spectral sequence (connective K-theory) with the KU-Adams spectral sequence (periodic K-theory); the former converges to connective covers, the latter to periodic phenomena.
  • Assuming the global Picard group Pic(𝒮) is the product of the local Picard groups; the chromatic fracture square imposes strict compatibility conditions that prevent most exotic local elements from lifting globally.
  • Believing the smashing conjecture holds for all localizations; it fails for Brown–Comenetz duality, and the classification of smashing localizations remains open globally.
  • Treating the Anderson dual I𝕊 as a smashing localization; it is not smashing, and the functor X ↦ F(X, I𝕊) does not commute with smash products.
  • Overlooking that the tmf Adams spectral sequence at p=2 uses the subalgebra A(2) of the Steenrod algebra, not the full Steenrod algebra, and that the descent spectral sequence for tmf is an Adams–Novikov type spectral sequence, not an Adams spectral sequence.
  • Assuming the exotic elements in the K(n)-local Picard group always correspond to global invertible spectra; the global Picard conjecture asserts they do not, and the fracture square provides the obstruction.
  • Neglecting that the Anderson dual of the sphere has homotopy groups π−1 = ℚ/ℤ and π0 = ℤ̂, and that Anderson self-duality of tmf and KO involves specific shifts (21 and 4 respectively) that are crucial for index theory applications.

The Atiyah–Hirzebruch Spectral Sequence for Generalized Cohomology

The Atiyah–Hirzebruch spectral sequence (AHSS) is the primary computational tool for evaluating a generalized cohomology theory E* on a CW-complex X. For a multiplicative cohomology theory represented by a ring spectrum E, the AHSS has E₂-page E₂p,q = H^p(X; E^q(pt)) converging to Ep+q(X). The differentials d_r: E_rp,q → E_rp+r, q-r+1 are stable cohomology operations in E-theory; the first nontrivial differential d₃ is often expressible in terms of Steenrod operations when E is complex K-theory or complex cobordism. Unlike the Serre spectral sequence, the AHSS is a first-quadrant spectral sequence for connective theories, but for periodic theories such as KU or MU it extends across all quadrants. The filtration on E*(X) comes from the skeletal filtration of X, and the edge homomorphism E^*(X) → H^*(X; E^*(pt)) is the map induced by the unit of the ring spectrum. A critical subtlety is that the AHSS does not always converge strongly; for non-connective spectra or infinite complexes, one must check the Mittag-Leffler condition on the inverse system of cohomology groups of the skeleta. The AHSS for real K-theory KO has E₂-page H^p(X; KO^q(pt)) where KO^*(pt) is 8-periodic with groups ℤ, ℤ/2, ℤ/2, 0, ℤ, 0, 0, 0 in degrees 0 through 7. The differentials are governed by the action of the Steenrod squares and the Bockstein on the coefficient system, and the first differential d₃ is given by Sq³ + Sq²Sq¹ on mod 2 cohomology. For complex cobordism MU, the AHSS collapses at E₂ for simply connected spaces because MU is complex orientable and the coefficient ring MU* is polynomial on generators in even degrees, forcing all differentials to vanish for degree reasons. The AHSS for Morava K-theory K(n) at prime p has E₂-page H^p(X; K(n)^q(pt)) where K(n)* = 𝔽_p[v_n±1] with |v_n| = 2(p^n-1); the differentials are related to the action of the Morava stabilizer group and are highly nontrivial. A standard application is the computation of KU*(ℂP^∞) where the AHSS collapses and yields KU*(ℂP^∞) ≅ KU*[[x]] with x in degree 2, while for KO*(ℂP^∞) the differentials produce the well-known 2-torsion pattern. The AHSS is also the starting point for the Adams–Novikov spectral sequence when E = MU, and its E₂-page is the cohomology of the moduli stack of formal groups.

The Bockstein Spectral Sequence and Cohomology with Arbitrary Coefficients

The Bockstein spectral sequence (BSS) arises from a short exact sequence of coefficient groups 0 → A → B → C → 0 and computes the cohomology H*(X; C) from H*(X; A) and H*(X; B). When the sequence is 0 → ℤ → ℤ → ℤ/p → 0, the associated long exact sequence in cohomology yields the Bockstein homomorphism β: H^q(X; ℤ/p) → Hq+1(X; ℤ/p), which satisfies β² = 0 for odd p but β² = Sq¹ for p = 2. Iterating this construction produces the Bockstein spectral sequence with E₁p,q = Hp+q(X; ℤ/p) and d₁ = β, converging to the p-torsion in H*(X; ℤ) completed at p. The E_∞-page gives the associated graded of the p-adic filtration on integral cohomology. For a general coefficient group G, the universal coefficient theorem gives a short exact sequence 0 → Ext(Hq-1(X), G) → H^q(X; G) → Hom(H_q(X), G) → 0, which splits non-naturally. The Bockstein spectral sequence for the tower of coefficient groups ℤ/p^k provides a systematic way to compute H*(X; ℤ_p) and H*(X; ℚ_p/ℤ_p). The differentials d_r in the BSS are related to the higher Bockstein operations β_r, which are cohomology operations of degree 1 defined on the kernel of βr-1. For p = 2, the Bockstein spectral sequence interacts with the Steenrod algebra: the operation Sq¹ coincides with β₁, and the Adem relations imply β_r Sq^I = Σ Sq^J β_s for appropriate sequences I, J. A key computational example is the cohomology of the Eilenberg–MacLane space K(ℤ, n): the BSS for the coefficient sequence 0 → ℤ → ℤ → ℤ/p → 0 computes H*(K(ℤ, n); ℤ/p) as a polynomial algebra on the Bockstein images of the fundamental class, yielding the classical result that H*(K(ℤ, n); ℤ/p) is generated by the iterated Bocksteins βε_r⋯βε1ι_n subject to the Adem relations. The BSS also underlies the construction of the Adams spectral sequence with coefficients in ℤ/p, where the E₂-page is ExtA(H*(X; ℤ/p), ℤ/p) and the differentials are detected by the Bockstein spectral sequence of the coefficient module. In the context of Morava K-theory, the Bockstein spectral sequence for the sequence 0 → K(n)* → K(n)* → K(n)*/p → 0 relates the cohomology operations in K(n)-theory to the action of the Morava stabilizer group on the Lubin–Tate deformation space.

Worked Computation: AHSS for KO*(ℝP⁴)
Compute KO*(ℝP⁴) using the Atiyah–Hirzebruch spectral sequence. The mod 2 cohomology of ℝP⁴ is H*(ℝP⁴; ℤ/2) = ℤ/2[x]/(x⁵) with |x| = 1. The coefficient ring KO*(pt) is 8-periodic: KO⁰ = ℤ, KO⁻¹ = KO⁻² = ℤ/2, KO⁻³ = 0, KO⁻⁴ = ℤ, KO⁻⁵ = KO⁻⁶ = KO⁻⁷ = 0. The E₂-page is E₂p,q = H^p(ℝP⁴; KO^q(pt)). Since KO^q(pt) is either ℤ, ℤ/2, or 0, the cohomology groups are either ℤ/2 (for p ≤ 4) or 0. The first potentially nontrivial differential is d₃: E₃p,q → E₃p+3, q-2. For KO-theory, d₃ is given by the operation Sq³ + Sq²Sq¹ on mod 2 cohomology with coefficients in the ℤ/2 summands. On H*(ℝP⁴; ℤ/2), Sq¹(x^i) = i xi+1, Sq²(x^i) = (i choose 2) xi+2, Sq³(x^i) = (i choose 3) xi+3. Compute d₃ on the generators in the q = -1, -2 rows (where coefficients are ℤ/2). For q = -1 (KO⁻¹ = ℤ/2), the generator in E₂p,-1 is x^p. Then d₃(x^p) = (Sq³ + Sq²Sq¹)(x^p) = (p choose 3)xp+3 + (p choose 2)(p+2 choose 1)xp+3 = [(p choose 3) + (p choose 2)(p+2)]xp+3 mod 2. For p = 0: d₃(1) = 0. For p = 1: d₃(x) = 0. For p = 2: d₃(x²) = (0 + 1·4)x⁵ = 0 (since x⁵ = 0 in ℝP⁴). For p = 3: d₃(x³) = (1 + 3·5)x⁶ = 0. Thus d₃ = 0 on all generators. Higher differentials d_r for r ≥ 4 have target beyond the 4-skeleton, so they vanish. The spectral sequence collapses at E₂. The E_∞-page gives the associated graded of the filtration on KO*(ℝP⁴). The extension problems are resolved by the known ring structure: KO⁰(ℝP⁴) = ℤ ⊕ ℤ/2 (generated by 1 and the reduced class of the canonical line bundle), KO⁻¹ = ℤ/2, KO⁻² = ℤ/2, KO⁻³ = 0, KO⁻⁴ = ℤ.
The AHSS for KO*(ℝP⁴) has E₂p,q = H^p(ℝP⁴; KO^q(pt)). KO^q(pt) is nonzero only for q ≡ 0, -1, -2, -4 mod 8. The mod 2 cohomology of ℝP⁴ is ℤ/2[x]/(x⁵), |x|=1. The E₂-page consists of ℤ/2 in positions (p,q) with 0 ≤ p ≤ 4 and q ≡ -1, -2 mod 8, and ℤ in positions (p,0) and (p,-4) for 0 ≤ p ≤ 4. The differential d₃ = Sq³ + Sq²Sq¹ acts on the ℤ/2 rows. On x^p, Sq³(x^p) = (p choose 3)xp+3, Sq²Sq¹(x^p) = (p choose 2)(p+2 choose 1)xp+3. Mod 2, (p choose 3) + (p choose 2)(p+2) vanishes for p = 0,1,2,3 (direct check). Thus d₃ = 0. All higher differentials vanish for degree reasons. The spectral sequence collapses at E₂. The filtration on KO⁰(ℝP⁴) has graded pieces ℤ (from p=0,q=0) and ℤ/2 (from p=1,q=-1, p=2,q=-2, p=4,q=-4). The extension yields KO⁰ = ℤ ⊕ ℤ/2, KO⁻¹ = ℤ/2, KO⁻² = ℤ/2, KO⁻³ = 0, KO⁻⁴ = ℤ.
Exam Strategy: Spectral Sequence Collapse Criteria
When asked to compute a generalized cohomology group via the AHSS, first check whether the coefficient ring E* is concentrated in even degrees (e.g., MU, KU, BP). If so, all differentials d_r for odd r vanish for degree reasons, and even differentials d2k map between groups of opposite parity in the total degree, often forcing collapse. For KO-theory, remember the 8-periodic coefficient pattern and that d₃ = Sq³ + Sq²Sq¹ on mod 2 rows. For the Bockstein spectral sequence, the key is to identify the coefficient sequence and compute the first differential β; higher differentials β_r are often determined by the Adem relations. Always verify convergence: for finite CW-complexes and connective theories, the AHSS converges strongly. For periodic theories on infinite complexes, check the lim¹ term via the Milnor exact sequence.

The Dyer–Lashof Operations and the Homology of Infinite Loop Spaces

The Dyer–Lashof operations are a family of homology operations Q^i (for p = 2) and Q^i, βQ^i (for odd p) acting on the homology H_*(Ω^n X; ℤ/p) of an n-fold loop space. They were introduced to compute the homology of the infinite symmetric product and the classifying spaces of the stable orthogonal and unitary groups. For an E_∞-space (an infinite loop space), the Dyer–Lashof operations satisfy the Adem relations, the Cartan formula, and the Nishida relations which describe their interaction with the Steenrod algebra operations on cohomology via the Kronecker pairing. The operations raise degree: |Q^i| = i(p-1) for odd p, and |Q^i| = i for p = 2. They are defined using the action of the little cubes operads C_n on n-fold loop spaces; the homology of C_n carries the Dyer–Lashof algebra as a quotient. A fundamental result is the Cohen–Lada–May recognition theorem: a connected space X is weakly equivalent to an n-fold loop space if and only if it admits an action of the little n-cubes operad C_n, and it is an infinite loop space if and only if it admits an action of the Barratt–Eccles operad or the linear isometries operad. The homology of the free E_∞-algebra on a space X is the polynomial algebra (for p = 2) or the tensor product of a polynomial and an exterior algebra (for odd p) generated by the Dyer–Lashof operations on the homology of X. This yields explicit descriptions of H_*(Ω^∞Σ^∞ X; ℤ/p) as the free Dyer–Lashof algebra on H_*(X; ℤ/p). For the sphere spectrum, H_*(QS⁰; ℤ/2) is the polynomial algebra on the Dyer–Lashof operations applied to the fundamental class, modulo the Adem relations. The Nishida relations state that for x ∈ H_*(X; ℤ/p) and a Steenrod operation P^s (or Sq^s), the pairing ⟨P^s, Q^i x⟩ is a sum of terms ⟨P^t, x⟩ Q^j. These relations are essential for computing the action of the Steenrod algebra on the cohomology of infinite loop spaces. The Dyer–Lashof operations also appear in the homology of the stable orthogonal group O = colim O(n): H_*(BO; ℤ/2) is the polynomial algebra on the Stiefel–Whitney classes, and the Dyer–Lashof operations on the dual homology classes give the Wu formula for the Steenrod squares of Stiefel–Whitney classes. In chromatic homotopy theory, the Dyer–Lashof operations on the Morava K-theory of E_∞-ring spectra are related to the power operations constructed by Ando, Hopkins, and Strickland, which are central to the Goerss–Hopkins obstruction theory for E_∞-structures.

The Cohen–Lada–May Recognition Principle for E_n-Algebras

The recognition principle, proved by Cohen, Lada, and May in the 1970s and extended by May and Boardman–Vogt, characterizes n-fold loop spaces as algebras over the little n-cubes operad C_n. The operad C_n(k) is the space of k-tuples of disjoint n-dimensional cubes linearly embedded in the unit cube (0,1)^n. An action of C_n on a space X gives it the structure of an E_n-algebra: a multiplication that is associative and commutative up to coherent homotopies of order n. The theorem states that for a connected space X, the following are equivalent: (1) X is weakly equivalent to an n-fold loop space Ω^n Y for some Y; (2) X admits an action of the little n-cubes operad C_n; (3) X is a grouplike E_n-algebra (i.e., π₀(X) is a group). For n = ∞, the little ∞-cubes operad is equivalent to the Barratt–Eccles operad or the linear isometries operad, and the theorem characterizes infinite loop spaces as grouplike E_∞-algebras. The proof constructs a monad C_n = Ω^n Σ^n and shows that the category of C_n-algebras is Quillen equivalent to the category of n-fold loop spaces. The homology of the free C_n-algebra on a space X is the free Poisson algebra (for n = 2) or the free E_n-algebra in the sense of operadic homology. For n = 1, C1 is the associative operad, and the theorem reduces to the classical fact that connected A_∞-spaces are loop spaces (Stasheff). For n = 2, the homology operations are the Browder bracket (a Lie bracket of degree n-1 = 1) and the Dyer–Lashof operations. The recognition principle is the foundation of higher algebra: it allows one to study E_n-ring spectra by studying algebras over the sphere spectrum in the ∞-category of spectra. The Dunn additivity theorem states that an E_n-algebra in E_m-algebras is an En+m-algebra, which reflects the equivalence C_n ⊗ C_m ≃ Cn+m. This underlies the Baez–Dolan stabilization hypothesis and the cobordism hypothesis. In derived algebraic geometry, the recognition principle for E_∞-algebras is used to define the moduli stack of E_∞-ring spectra and to formulate the Goerss–Hopkins obstruction theory for lifting a homotopy commutative ring spectrum to an E_∞-structure. The obstruction groups are André–Quillen cohomology groups of the E_∞-algebra, which can be computed using the cotangent complex in the ∞-category of E_∞-ring spectra.

Motivic Homotopy Theory and the A¹-Homotopy Category

Motivic homotopy theory, developed by Morel and Voevodsky in the 1990s, constructs a homotopy category of schemes over a base field k that mimics the classical homotopy category of topological spaces. The key idea is to treat the affine line A¹ as the interval object, making A¹-homotopic maps equivalent. The category of smooth schemes over k is equipped with the Nisnevich topology, and one considers the category of simplicial sheaves on the Nisnevich site. The A¹-homotopy category H(k) is obtained by localizing the model category of simplicial sheaves with respect to the A¹-weak equivalences: maps that become weak equivalences after taking the product with A¹. The spheres in this category are the simplicial sphere S^s and the Tate sphere S^t = (G_m)∧t, where G_m = A¹ {0}. The motivic stable homotopy category SH(k) is the stabilization of H(k) with respect to the Tate sphere. The motivic cohomology groups Hp,q(X, ℤ) are defined as HomSH(k)(Σ^∞_T X_+, Sp,q ∧ Hℤ) where Sp,q = Sp-q ∧ (G_m)∧q. Voevodsky proved that motivic cohomology with finite coefficients satisfies the Beilinson–Lichtenbaum conjecture (now a theorem): the map from motivic cohomology to étale cohomology is an isomorphism in degrees p ≤ q and injective in degree p = q+1. This implies the norm residue isomorphism theorem (the Bloch–Kato conjecture), which identifies the Galois cohomology of a field with its Milnor K-theory modulo p. The motivic Steenrod algebra acts on motivic cohomology, and the motivic Adams spectral sequence converges to the motivic stable homotopy groups of spheres. Over ℂ, the Betti realization functor SH(ℂ) → SH (classical stable homotopy category) is a symmetric monoidal functor that sends the motivic sphere to the classical sphere and the Tate sphere to the circle. The motivic Hopf map η ∈ π1,1(S⁰) maps to the classical Hopf map η ∈ π₁(S⁰). The motivic stable homotopy groups of spheres over ℂ are known to be related to the classical stable stems via the η-inverted Adams–Novikov spectral sequence. The Morel–Voevodsky A¹-homotopy theory also provides a framework for the six operations formalism (f_*, f^*, f_!, f^!, ⊗, Hom) in étale cohomology, which is essential for the proof of the Weil conjectures and for the geometric Langlands program. The category of motives DM(k) is the full subcategory of SH(k) generated by the motives of smooth schemes, and it carries a t-structure whose heart is the category of effective motives. The realization functors to Hodge structures, ℓ-adic cohomology, and de Rham cohomology are all symmetric monoidal and compatible with the six operations.

  • Confusing the Atiyah–Hirzebruch spectral sequence (which filters by skeleta of the space) with the Adams spectral sequence (which filters by the sphere spectrum); the AHSS has E₂ = H*(X; E*(pt)), while the Adams SS has E₂ = ExtE*E(E*, E*X).
  • Assuming the AHSS always collapses for complex orientable theories; it collapses for MU on simply connected spaces, but for KO or KU the differentials are nontrivial and must be computed using Steenrod operations.
  • Overlooking that the Bockstein spectral sequence for ℤ/p coefficients converges to the p-completion of integral cohomology, not to integral cohomology itself; the lim¹ term captures the difference.
  • Misapplying the Nishida relations by forgetting the sign conventions for odd primes; the relations involve the action of the Steenrod algebra on the Dyer–Lashof operations and are essential for computing the homology of infinite loop spaces.
  • Believing the recognition principle applies to non-connected spaces without group completion; the theorem requires the space to be grouplike (π₀ is a group), otherwise one only gets an equivalence after group completion.
  • Confusing the little n-cubes operad C_n with the E_n-operad in spectra; C_n acts on spaces, while the spectral E_n-operad acts on spectra, and the recognition principle for spectra involves the sphere spectrum and the ∞-category of spectra.
  • Neglecting that motivic homotopy theory over a general base field requires the Nisnevich topology and A¹-localization; the Zariski topology is insufficient for descent, and the A¹-homotopy category is not the same as the classical homotopy category of the associated complex points.

The Adams Spectral Sequence for Connective K-Theory and the Image of J

The Adams spectral sequence based on connective complex K-theory ku (and its real analogue ko) provides a computational framework for the image of the J-homomorphism that is more accessible than the classical Adams spectral sequence at the prime 2. The ku-Adams spectral sequence has E2-page Extku*ku(ku*, ku*X) converging to the ku-completion of the stable homotopy groups of X. For the sphere spectrum, the E2-term was computed by Adams and Priddy in the 1970s using the isomorphism ku*ku ≅ ku*[b1, b2, …] where |bi| = 2i. The resulting Ext groups are concentrated in even topological degrees, forcing the spectral sequence to collapse at E2 for the sphere. This collapse identifies the ku-completion of π*(S) with a polynomial algebra on certain generators, and the image of J appears as the subgroup generated by the ku-Bott element and the classes detected by the bi. Mahowald used this to give a geometric construction of the image of J via the Thom spectrum of the map BO → BGL1(S) classifying the spherical fibration associated to the virtual bundle ξ − dim ξ. The ko-Adams spectral sequence at the prime 2, developed by Mahowald and others, refines this by incorporating the real Bott periodicity of period 8. Its E2-page involves Ext over ko*ko, which is more complicated due to the presence of η and η2 in ko*. The differentials in the ko-Adams spectral sequence are closely related to the Adams differentials via the map ko → Hℤ/2, and the detection of the image of J in the 2-primary stable stems is achieved by comparing the ko- and ku-based spectral sequences. The Adams conjecture, proved by Quillen and Sullivan, asserts that the map BU → BGL1(S) becomes null after Adams operations, and the ku-Adams spectral sequence provides the natural home for this statement: the Adams operations act on ku and induce operations on the spectral sequence that kill the image of J in the fiber of ψk − 1.

The Classification of Thick Subcategories in the K(n)-Local Stable Homotopy Category

The thick subcategory theorem of Hopkins and Smith (1998) classifies thick subcategories of the category of finite spectra: they are in bijection with subsets of {0, 1, 2, …, ∞} via the Morava K-theories K(n). In the K(n)-local stable homotopy category SpK(n), the analogous classification is more subtle and remains an active area of research. A thick subcategory of SpK(n) is a full triangulated subcategory closed under retracts. The telescope conjecture for K(n)-local spectra, which would identify the Bousfield class of the telescope of a vn-self-map with that of K(n), is known to fail for n ≥ 2 (by work of Ravenel, and more recently Burklund–Hahn–Levy–Schlank). This failure implies that the lattice of Bousfield classes in SpK(n) is richer than in the global category. The classification of thick subcategories of K(n)-local finite spectra (i.e., K(n)-localizations of finite spectra) is known to correspond to the height filtration: the only thick subcategories are 0 and the whole category, because K(n)-local finite spectra are all K(n)-local and K(m)-acyclic for m ≠ n. However, for general K(n)-local spectra, the situation is more complex. The work of Hovey and Strickland on the category of K(n)-local spectra shows that it is a well-generated tensor-triangulated category, and the Balmer spectrum of its compact objects is a single point. For non-compact objects, the classification of localizing subcategories (closed under arbitrary coproducts) is known by work of Barthel–Heard–Valenzuela: they are in bijection with subsets of the set of K(n)-localizing invariants, which for n = 1 is related to the p-adic integers, and for n ≥ 2 involves the Morava stabilizer group Gn. The thick subcategories of the compact objects in SpK(n) are trivial, but the thick subcategories of the category of K(n)-local spectra that are dualizable (i.e., the K(n)-local finite spectra) are also trivial. The interesting question is the classification of thick subcategories of the category of K(n)-local spectra that are not necessarily dualizable. This is related to the chromatic splitting conjecture, which predicts a decomposition of the K(n)-local sphere into a wedge of suspensions of EnhG for various subgroups G ⊂ Gn. The thick subcategories generated by these summands would then correspond to the lattice of closed subgroups of Gn, providing a Galois-theoretic classification analogous to the global case but with the Morava stabilizer group replacing the integers.

Worked Example: Detecting the Image of J in the ku-Adams Spectral Sequence
Show that the class α1 ∈ π3(S)(2) (the first Hopf map η at the prime 2) is in the image of J using the ku-Adams spectral sequence.
The ku-Adams spectral sequence for the sphere has E2s,w = Extku*kus,w(ku*, ku*). The algebra ku*ku ≅ ku*[b1, b2, …] with |bi| = 2i. The cobar complex computes Ext. In topological degree t = w − s = 3, we look for classes. The generator b1 has degree (1, 2) in (s, w) bidegree, so t = 1. The class η ∈ π1(S) corresponds to b1 in the E2-page. The class α1 = η2 ∈ π2(S)? Wait: η ∈ π1, η2 ∈ π2, but α1 ∈ π3 is the class detected by h12 in the classical Adams SS. In the ku-Adams SS, the class α1 is detected by b12 in Ext2,4 (since |b1| = (1,2), so b12 has (s,w) = (2,4), giving t = 2). But α1 ∈ π3? Actually α1 = η3? No: π3(S)(2) = ℤ/8 generated by ν, and η3 = 4ν. The image of J in π3 is ℤ/8. In the ku-Adams SS, the image of J is generated by the ku-Bott element β ∈ π2(ku) and the classes from the bi. The map J: π3(SO) → π3(S) sends the generator to ν. The ku-Adams SS collapses at E2, so ν is detected by a permanent cycle. The class ν corresponds to the element b2 − b12 in Ext1,4 (since |b2| = (1,4)). This is a permanent cycle because the SS collapses. The image of J in the ku-Adams E∞-page is the subalgebra generated by the bi and the Bott element. Since b2 − b12 is in this subalgebra, ν is in the image of J.
Exam Strategy: Spectral Sequence Comparison
When asked to compare the classical Adams SS, the Adams-Novikov SS, and the ku/ko-Adams SS, focus on three axes: (1) the coefficient ring (Hℤ/p, MU, ku/ko), (2) the Hopf algebroid governing the E2-page (Steenrod algebra, (MU*, MU*MU), (ku*, ku*ku)), and (3) the convergence target (p-completion, MU-completion, ku/ko-completion). The ku-Adams SS collapses for the sphere; the classical Adams SS does not. The image of J is visible in ku-Adams but requires Toda brackets in classical Adams. Memorize the collapse theorem: ku-Adams SS for S collapses at E2 because ku*ku is polynomial on even-degree generators.

The Adams-Novikov Spectral Sequence for Topological Modular Forms

The Adams-Novikov spectral sequence (ANSS) for topological modular forms tmf is a central tool in modern chromatic homotopy theory, connecting the homotopy groups of tmf to the cohomology of the moduli stack of elliptic curves Mell. The E2-page of the ANSS for tmf is given by E2s,w = Ext(tmf*, tmf*tmf)s,w(tmf*, tmf*) ≅ Hs(Mell; ωw/2), where ω is the line bundle of invariant differentials on the universal elliptic curve. This isomorphism, due to Goerss–Hopkins–Miller, identifies the tmf-Adams-Novikov E2-page with the sheaf cohomology of Mell with coefficients in powers of ω. The compactified moduli stack M̄ell includes the cusp, and the cohomology of M̄ell computes the E2-page for periodic tmf (i.e., Tmf). The differentials in this spectral sequence are governed by the geometry of Mell: the d3 differential is related to the Hasse invariant, and higher differentials correspond to modular forms with level structure. The computation of π*(tmf) at primes p ≥ 5 was carried out by Behrens and others using the ANSS, revealing a pattern of v1- and v2-periodic families. At p = 2 and 3, the ANSS for tmf is more complicated due to the non-smoothness of Mell at supersingular points. The work of Hill–Hopkins–Ravenel on the Kervaire invariant problem used a version of the ANSS for tmf with level structure to construct the spectrum tmf1(3) and detect the Kervaire invariant elements. The string orientation of tmf, constructed by Ando–Hopkins–Rezk–Strickland, gives a map of ring spectra MString → tmf, and the induced map on ANSS E2-pages corresponds to the map on moduli stacks MString → Mell classifying the elliptic curve associated to a string manifold. The Witten genus, which takes values in modular forms, is recovered as the edge homomorphism of the ANSS for tmf. The ANSS for tmf also provides a framework for the chromatic fracture square at height 2: the K(2)-localization of tmf is related to Morava E-theory E2 via the Goerss–Hopkins–Miller theorem, and the ANSS for tmf interpolates between the K(1)-local and K(2)-local information.

The Telescope Conjecture for the K(n)-Local Category

The telescope conjecture in its original form, proposed by Ravenel in 1984, states that for any finite spectrum X with a vn-self-map v, the Bousfield class of the telescope T(v) equals the Bousfield class of the Morava K-theory K(n). This was known to hold for n = 0, 1 (by work of Miller, Ravenel, and others) but was disproved for n ≥ 2 by Ravenel (1992) using the theory of vn-periodic elements and the chromatic spectral sequence. The K(n)-local version of the telescope conjecture asks whether, in the K(n)-local stable homotopy category SpK(n), the telescope of a vn-self-map on a K(n)-local finite spectrum is equivalent to the K(n)-local sphere. This is equivalent to asking whether the K(n)-localization of the telescope T(v) is the K(n)-local sphere.
