The failure of the global telescope conjecture for n ≥ 2 implies that the K(n)-local telescope conjecture also fails, because if LK(n)T(v) ≃ LK(n)S, then T(v) would be K(n)-local and hence Bousfield equivalent to K(n), contradicting Ravenel's counterexample. However, the K(n)-local telescope conjecture has a more refined formulation due to Hovey and Strickland: the category of K(n)-local spectra is a tensor-triangulated category with a unique non-trivial thick subcategory of compact objects (the K(n)-local finite spectra). The telescope conjecture for K(n)-local spectra would imply that the Bousfield lattice of SpK(n) is trivial (only 0 and the whole category). The work of Barthel–Heard–Valenzuela on the classification of localizing subcategories shows that the Bousfield lattice of SpK(n) is in fact non-trivial for n ≥ 1, with localizing subcategories corresponding to closed subgroups of the Morava stabilizer group Gn. The recent breakthrough by Burklund, Hahn, Levy, and Schlank (2023) proved that the telescope conjecture fails for all n ≥ 2 and all primes, by constructing a non-trivial element in the K(n)-local sphere that is not detected by any telescope. Their proof uses the synthetic approach to chromatic homotopy theory, where the K(n)-local category is modeled by a category of synthetic spectra with a formal vn-self-map, and the telescope conjecture translates to a statement about the vanishing of certain Ext groups in this synthetic category. The failure of the telescope conjecture has profound implications for the structure of the stable homotopy category: it means that the chromatic filtration does not split in the way Ravenel originally hoped, and that the K(n)-local category contains more information than just the telescopes of vn-self-maps.

The Redshift Conjecture and the Algebraic K-Theory of Structured Ring Spectra

The redshift conjecture, formulated by Rognes and refined by Ausoni–Rognes, predicts a systematic increase in chromatic height when passing from a ring spectrum R to its algebraic K-theory K(R). Specifically, if R is a connective ring spectrum of chromatic height n (e.g., R = ku for n = 1, R = Ell for n = 2), then K(R) should have chromatic height n + 1. The conjecture is supported by computations: K(ℤ) has K(1)-local information (by Quillen–Lichtenbaum), K(ku) has K(2)-local information (by Ausoni–Rognes, who computed K(ku) at p = 2 and found v2-periodic families), and K(Ell) is expected to have K(3)-local information.

The Spanier-Whitehead Duality and the Wirthmüller Isomorphism

Spanier-Whitehead duality extends Poincaré duality from smooth manifolds to the stable homotopy category. For a finite CW-complex X, its Spanier-Whitehead dual is the spectrum DX = F(X, S), the function spectrum from X to the sphere spectrum S. The duality pairing X ∧ DX → S induces an isomorphism [Y, X] ≅ [Y ∧ DX, S] for any spectrum Y, identifying maps into X with maps out of its dual. This equivalence is natural and symmetric: D(DX) ≃ X. The construction relies on the fact that finite spectra are dualizable objects in the symmetric monoidal stable homotopy category. For a smooth closed manifold M embedded in ℝⁿ, the Pontryagin-Thom construction identifies the suspension spectrum Σ^∞ M⁺ with the Thom spectrum of the normal bundle, and Spanier-Whitehead duality recovers the classical Poincaré duality isomorphism H^k(M) ≅ Hn-k(M). The Wirthmüller isomorphism generalizes this to parametrized spectra. Given a map f: X → Y of spaces, the six-functor formalism provides adjoint pairs (f_!, f^!) and (f^*, f_*) between the categories of parametrized spectra over X and Y. When f is a proper submersion of smooth manifolds, the Wirthmüller isomorphism states that f_! ≃ f_* ⊗ ω_f, where ω_f is the relative dualizing object (the Thom spectrum of the vertical tangent bundle). In the stable homotopy category, this becomes an equivalence between the left and right adjoints of the pullback functor, twisted by the dualizing object. For a finite covering map, ω_f is trivial and f_! ≃ f_*, recovering the classical transfer map. The Wirthmüller isomorphism is essential for the construction of the transfer in generalized cohomology theories and for the proof of the Adams isomorphism in equivariant stable homotopy theory. It also underlies the duality theory for the stable homotopy category of G-spectra, where the dualizing object for a finite group G is the sphere spectrum with the sign representation.

The Galois Theory of Structured Ring Spectra

Rognes developed a Galois theory for structured ring spectra that parallels classical Galois theory for field extensions. A map of ring spectra A → B is a G-Galois extension for a finite group G if: (1) B is a faithful A-module, (2) the map B ∧_A B → ∏_G B induced by the G-action is an equivalence, and (3) the homotopy fixed points BhG ≃ A. This definition captures the essence of descent: the category of A-modules is equivalent to the category of B-modules with semilinear G-action. The primary example is the map from connective complex K-theory ku to the periodic complex K-theory KU, which is a ℤ/2-Galois extension where the generator acts by complex conjugation. Another fundamental example is the map from the sphere spectrum S to the connective Adams summand ℓ at a prime p, which is a Galois extension for the group of units in the p-adic integers. The theory provides a classification of Galois extensions in terms of the étale site of the ring spectrum, where the étale topology is defined by maps that induce étale extensions on homotopy groups. Rognes proved that for a connective ring spectrum A with π0(A) a field, the Galois group of the maximal Galois extension is the absolute Galois group of π0(A). The theory has been extended to ∞-categories of modules over E_∞-ring spectra by Mathew and others, where the Galois correspondence relates intermediate extensions to closed subgroups of the Galois group. This framework has applications to the computation of topological Hochschild homology via the cyclotomic trace, where the Galois descent for the map THH(A) → THH(B) plays a crucial role. It also underlies the approach to the telescope conjecture via the Galois theory of the K(n)-local sphere, where the Morava stabilizer group acts as a profinite Galois group on the Lubin-Tate theory E_n.

Worked Example: Verifying a Galois Extension
Show that the map ku → KU of connective to periodic complex K-theory is a ℤ/2-Galois extension. Identify the action of the generator and verify the three conditions.
The map ku → KU is the localization inverting the Bott element β ∈ π2(ku). The group ℤ/2 acts on KU by complex conjugation on the representing space BU × ℤ, which induces the action on homotopy groups: the generator sends β to -β. Condition (1): KU is a faithful ku-module because π_*(KU) = ℤ[β, β-1] is a free module over π_*(ku) = ℤ[β]. Condition (2): The map KU ∧ku KU → KU × KU is induced by the two maps KU → KU given by the identity and the conjugation action. On homotopy groups, this is ℤ[β, β-1] ⊗ℤ[β] ℤ[β, β-1] → ℤ[β, β-1] × ℤ[β, β-1], which is an isomorphism because β is inverted. Condition (3): The homotopy fixed points KUhℤ/2 have homotopy groups the group cohomology H^*(ℤ/2; π_*(KU)). Since the action on β is by -1, the invariant subring is ℤ[β2] = π_*(ku). The higher cohomology vanishes because the module is induced, so KUhℤ/2 ≃ ku.
Exam Strategy: Recognizing Galois Extensions
When a question asks about Galois extensions of ring spectra, check three things: (1) the map is faithful, (2) the Amitsur complex B ∧_A B ⇉ B is a split coequalizer (equivalent to the product over G), and (3) the homotopy fixed points recover the base. The standard examples are ku → KU (ℤ/2), S → ℓ (ℤ_p^×), and E_nhG_n → E_n (Morava stabilizer group). Know that the Galois group of the maximal extension of a connective ring spectrum with field π0 is the absolute Galois group of that field.

The Picard Group of the Stable Homotopy Category

The Picard group Pic(Sp) of the stable homotopy category consists of isomorphism classes of invertible spectra under the smash product, with the sphere spectrum S as the identity. An invertible spectrum X satisfies X ∧ Y ≃ S for some Y; such spectra are precisely the finite spectra that are dualizable and have a dual that is also their inverse. The Picard group fits into an exact sequence 0 → ℤ → Pic(Sp) → Pic(Sp)tors → 0, where the ℤ factor is generated by the suspension ΣS, and the torsion subgroup classifies invertible spectra of degree zero. The image of the J-homomorphism provides elements in the torsion subgroup: for each n, the fiber of the map S → KO given by the J-homomorphism in degree n yields an invertible spectrum. The Adams conjecture implies that the image of J in the stable stems corresponds to the torsion in Pic(Sp). The K(n)-local Picard group Pic(SpK(n)) is much larger and has been computed by Hopkins, Mahowald, and Sadofsky: it is isomorphic to ℤ_p × ℤ/(p^n-1) × ℤ/(p-1) for odd primes, with a similar description at p=2. The global Picard group injects into the product of the K(n)-local Picard groups via the chromatic fracture square. The Picard group acts on the category of modules over any ring spectrum R by smashing with invertible R-modules, and this action is used to define the Picard-graded homotopy groups of R. The computation of Pic(Sp) is related to the classification of spherical fibrations and the stable homotopy groups of spheres via the map from the stable stems to Pic(Sp) sending an element α: S^k → S to the mapping cone of α. The kernel of this map consists of elements that are nilpotent under the smash product, linking the Picard group to the nilpotence theorem.

The Motivic Homotopy Theory and the A¹-Homotopy Category

Morel and Voevodsky constructed the A¹-homotopy category SH(k) over a base field k as a universal receptacle for algebraic varieties with a homotopy theory that treats the affine line A¹ as contractible. The category is built from the category of smooth schemes over k by first inverting Nisnevich weak equivalences (maps inducing isomorphisms on stalks for the Nisnevich topology) and then localizing with respect to the projection X × A¹ → X. The resulting ∞-category is a stable, symmetric monoidal category with a six-functor formalism (f_!, f^!, f^*, f_*) for separated maps of finite type. The sphere spectrum in SH(k) is the suspension spectrum of the point, and its endomorphisms are the motivic stable homotopy groups of the base field, πp,q(S_k) = [Σp,q S_k, S_k], where Σp,q = Σp-q ∧ (G_m)∧q uses the simplicial and Tate suspensions. The motivic Steenrod algebra acts on motivic cohomology Hp,q(X, ℤ/ℓ), which is defined as HomSH(k)(Σp,q X, Hℤ/ℓ) for the motivic Eilenberg-MacLane spectrum. Voevodsky proved that motivic cohomology with finite coefficients satisfies the Beilinson-Lichtenbaum conjecture: the map to étale cohomology is an isomorphism in weights ≤ p and injective in weight p+1. This implies the norm residue isomorphism theorem (the Bloch-Kato conjecture), identifying the Galois cohomology of the field with the motivic cohomology of its spectrum. The slice filtration on SH(k) provides a spectral sequence converging to the motivic stable homotopy groups, with E1-page given by the motivic cohomology of the slices. The zero slice of the sphere spectrum is the motivic Eilenberg-MacLane spectrum, and the higher slices are related to the motivic Steenrod algebra. The A¹-connectedness theorem states that the A¹-fundamental group of a smooth proper variety is a birational invariant, and the rigidity theorem implies that the motivic homotopy type of a smooth scheme over a separably closed field is determined by its dimension. The six-functor formalism satisfies the purity isomorphism f^! ≃ f^* ⊗ Th(T_f) for smooth maps, where Th(T_f) is the Thom space of the relative tangent bundle, generalizing the Wirthmüller isomorphism to the motivic setting.

The Six-Functor Formalism in Stable Homotopy Theory

The six-functor formalism provides a coherent framework for the functoriality of cohomology theories with respect to maps of spaces or schemes. For a map f: X → Y in a suitable category (topological spaces, schemes, or spectra), there are four functors between the associated stable homotopy categories: f^*: SH(Y) → SH(X) (pullback), f_*: SH(X) → SH(Y) (pushforward), f_!: SH(X) → SH(Y) (pushforward with compact support), and f^!: SH(Y) → SH(X) (exceptional pullback). These satisfy adjunctions (f_!, f^!) and (f^*, f_*), and for a Cartesian square, the base change isomorphism f^* g_! ≃ g'_! f'^* holds. In the stable homotopy category of spectra, for a map of spaces f: X → Y, the functors are defined on parametrized spectra: f^* is pullback, f_* is the right adjoint (sections), f_! is the left adjoint (compactly supported sections), and f^! is the right adjoint of f_!. When f is a proper map, f_! ≃ f_*; when f is an open inclusion, f^! ≃ f^*. The Wirthmüller isomorphism f_! ≃ f_* ⊗ ω_f for a smooth proper map identifies the dualizing object ω_f as the Thom spectrum of the vertical tangent bundle. In the motivic stable homotopy category SH(k), the six functors exist for all separated maps of finite type between smooth schemes, and the purity isomorphism f^! ≃ f^* ⊗ Th(T_f) holds for smooth maps. The formalism extends to the étale topology, where the functors are defined on the derived category of étale sheaves, and to the category of motives, where they are constructed via the theory of correspondences. The six-functor formalism is essential for the statement and proof of Poincaré duality in generalized cohomology theories, for the construction of the trace map in the Grothendieck-Riemann-Roch theorem, and for the formulation of the Langlands correspondence in the geometric setting. In chromatic homotopy theory, the six-functor formalism for the K(n)-local category relates the functors on the category of K(n)-local spectra to the action of the Morava stabilizer group on the Lubin-Tate theory.

  • Trap: Confusing the Wirthmüller isomorphism f_! ≃ f_* ⊗ ω_f with the statement f_! ≃ f_*; the latter holds only for proper maps where ω_f is trivial.
  • Trap: Assuming every map of ring spectra is a Galois extension; the conditions (faithfulness, Amitsur complex, homotopy fixed points) are restrictive and fail for most localizations.
  • Trap: Thinking the global Picard group Pic(Sp) is the same as the K(n)-local Picard groups; the global group injects into the product but is much smaller.
  • Trap: Treating the A¹-homotopy category as just the classical homotopy category of schemes; the Nisnevich localization and A¹-localization are both essential and change the homotopy type drastically.
  • Trap: Forgetting that the six-functor formalism requires the map to be separated and of finite type in the motivic setting; it does not hold for arbitrary maps of schemes.
  • Trap: Confusing the motivic Steenrod algebra with the classical Steenrod algebra; the motivic version is bigger, with extra generators τ and ρ, and acts on bigraded motivic cohomology.
  • Trap: Assuming the slice spectral sequence converges strongly to the motivic stable homotopy groups without checking the vanishing of the slices in negative weights.

These advanced topics form the backbone of modern research in algebraic topology and its interfaces with arithmetic geometry, representation theory, and mathematical physics.

The Atiyah–Hirzebruch Spectral Sequence and Generalized Cohomology

The Atiyah–Hirzebruch spectral sequence (AHSS) is the primary computational tool for evaluating a generalized cohomology theory E^* on a CW‑complex X. It arises from the skeletal filtration of X and has E2-page E2p,q=H^p(X;E^q(pt)) converging to Ep+q(X). The differentials d_r have bidegree (r,1-r) and are natural with respect to maps of spaces and of cohomology theories. When E is complex K‑theory, the E2-page is ordinary cohomology with coefficients in Z (even degrees) and 0 (odd degrees); the first non‑trivial differential is d3=operatorname{Sq}3 composed with the Bockstein, reflecting the fact that the third integral Stiefel‑Whitney class obstructs a spin^c structure. For complex cobordism MU, the AHSS collapses at E2 for any space whose ordinary cohomology is torsion‑free, giving a direct link between the Lazard ring and the formal group law of MU. The spectral sequence is multiplicative: the product on the E2-page is the cup product in ordinary cohomology tensored with the ring structure of E^*(pt), and the differentials satisfy a Leibniz rule. Convergence is strong for finite CW‑complexes; for infinite complexes one must check the lim1 term arising from the inverse limit of the filtration quotients. A standard application is the computation of KO^*( RP^n ) where the differentials encode the action of the Steenrod squares on the coefficient ring Z[eta,α,β]/(2eta,eta3,etaα,α2-4β). The AHSS also underlies the proof of the Conner–Floyd isomorphism MU^*(X)⊗MU^* Z≅ K^*(X) after inverting the Bott element.

The James Construction and the Homology of Loop Spaces

The James construction JX provides a functorial model for the free monoid on a based space X and a homotopy equivalence JX≃ ΩΣ X when X is a connected CW‑complex. The filtration by word length yields a spectral sequence whose E1-page is the tensor algebra on the reduced homology tilde H_*(X); this is the homology version of the Bott–Samelson theorem. In particular, for a sphere S^n the homology of Ω Sn+1 is a polynomial algebra on a generator in degree n when n is even, and an exterior algebra tensored with a divided power algebra when n is odd. The James map JX→ ΩΣ X induces an isomorphism on homology with any coefficients, and the Pontryagin product on H_*(ΩΣ X) corresponds to concatenation of words. The construction also yields the James splitting ΣΩΣ X≃ bigveek≥1Σ Xwedge k, which after applying a reduced homology theory E_* gives a decomposition of E_*(ΩΣ X) into a direct sum of the E_*-homology of the smash powers. This splitting is the geometric origin of the Kahn–Priddy theorem on the stable homotopy of Ω^∞ S^∞ and of the Snaith splitting for Ω^nΣ^n X. The James construction is natural with respect to maps of spaces and respects the action of the Steenrod algebra on mod‑p homology, making it a key ingredient in the proof of the Cohen–Moore–Neisendorfer exponent theorem for odd primary homotopy groups of spheres.

Worked Example: Computing H_*(Ω S3;Z) via the James Construction
Use the James filtration to determine the integral homology of the loop space on the 3‑sphere.

The James construction gives a filtration J_k S2 with J_k S2/Jk-1S2≃ (S2)wedge k. Since S2 is a 2‑sphere, its reduced homology is Z in degree 2 and zero elsewhere. The smash power (S2)wedge k is a 2k-sphere, so its reduced homology is Z in degree 2k. The associated spectral sequence has E1k,2k=Z and all other entries zero; there are no possible differentials for degree reasons. Hence the spectral sequence collapses at E1 and H_*(Ω S3;Z) is a free graded commutative algebra on one generator x of degree 2. Because the Pontryagin product corresponds to concatenation, the algebra is the polynomial algebra Z[x] with |x|=2. This matches the known result that Ω S3 has the homology of a polynomial algebra on a degree‑2 class.

Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks for the homology of a loop space on a sphere, first identify the dimension of the sphere. If the sphere is even‑dimensional, the answer is a polynomial algebra on a generator of degree n; if odd, it is an exterior algebra on a degree n generator tensored with a divided power algebra on a degree 2n generator. Write down the James filtration and note that the spectral sequence collapses for degree reasons — this justification earns full marks.

Configuration Spaces, Scanning Maps, and Homological Stability

For a connected manifold M of dimension at least 2, the unordered configuration space C_k(M) of k distinct points carries a natural map C_k(M)→ Ck+1(M) by adding a point near a fixed basepoint in the complement of a large ball. The homology groups H_i(C_k(M);Z) stabilize as k→∞ for each fixed i; this is the homological stability theorem of McDuff and Segal. The stable homology is identified with the homology of the infinite loop space Ω^∞0 Σ^∞ M_+ via the scanning map C_k(M)→ Ω^k S^k (when M=R^n) or more generally C_k(M)→ Γ_c(TM), the space of compactly supported sections of the tangent bundle. The scanning map is a homology equivalence in a range that grows linearly with k. For M=R^n the stable homology is the homology of the n-fold loop space on the n-sphere, which by the James construction is a free graded commutative algebra on the reduced homology of Sn-1. The stability range is sharp: H_i(C_k(M)) is independent of k for k>2i when dim M≥ 2. The proof uses the semi‑simplicial resolution of the configuration space by ordered configurations and the spectral sequence of a simplicial space, together with the high connectivity of the “forgetful” maps. This machinery extends to configuration spaces with labels in a connected space X, yielding the group completion theorem: the homology of the monoid coprod_k C_k(M)×Σ_k X^k after group completion is the homology of Ω^nΣ^n (M_+wedge X). Applications include the computation of the homology of mapping class groups via the Harer–Ivanov stability theorem and the identification of the Madsen–Weiss theorem on the cohomology of the moduli space of Riemann surfaces.

The Landweber Exact Functor Theorem and Complex Oriented Cohomology Theories

A complex oriented cohomology theory E is a multiplicative generalized cohomology theory equipped with a choice of Thom class for complex vector bundles, equivalently a map of ring spectra MU→ E. The coefficient ring E^* then carries a formal group law F_E over E^* obtained from the first Chern class of the tensor product of line bundles. The Landweber exact functor theorem gives a criterion for when a graded ring R equipped with a formal group law F defines a homology theory via X↦ MU_*(X)⊗MU_* R. The condition is that for each prime p the sequence (p, v1, v2, …) is a regular sequence in R, where v_i are the Hazewinkel generators of the universal p-typical formal group law. When the condition holds, the functor is exact on the category of MU_*-modules and therefore extends to a homology theory represented by a ring spectrum E. Classic examples include complex K‑theory (where the formal group law is the multiplicative one, v1 is the Bott element, and the regularity condition is satisfied after inverting v1), elliptic cohomology (where the formal group law comes from an elliptic curve and the sequence is regular away from the discriminant), and the Morava E-theories E_n (where the formal group law is the universal deformation of a height n formal group over a perfect field of characteristic p). The theorem also underlies the construction of the topological modular forms spectrum tmf via a compactified moduli stack of elliptic curves and a sheaf of E_∞ ring spectra satisfying the Landweber exactness condition on each affine open. A common pitfall is to assume that any formal group law over a ring yields a cohomology theory; the regularity condition is essential and fails for the additive formal group law over Z (which would give ordinary homology, not a complex oriented theory). The Landweber exact functor theorem is the algebraic gateway from formal groups to stable homotopy theory and is a prerequisite for understanding the chromatic filtration.

  • Trap: Assuming the Atiyah–Hirzebruch spectral sequence always collapses at E2; non‑trivial differentials appear whenever the coefficient ring has torsion or the space has non‑zero Steenrod operations.
  • Trap: Confusing the James construction JX with the free loop space LX; JX models ΩΣ X, not XS1.
  • Trap: Forgetting that homological stability for configuration spaces requires dim M≥ 2; for surfaces the stability range is k>2i but fails for 1‑manifolds.
  • Trap: Applying the Landweber exact functor theorem without checking the regularity of the sequence (p, v1, v2, …) at each prime.
  • Trap: Treating the scanning map as a homotopy equivalence on the nose; it is only a homology equivalence in a range and becomes an equivalence after group completion.
  • Trap: Overlooking the lim1 term in the AHSS for infinite CW‑complexes, which can produce exotic extensions not visible on the E_∞-page.
  • Trap: Assuming the formal group law of a complex oriented theory is always Landweber exact; many formal group laws (e.g., the additive one over Z) fail the regularity condition.

These sections complete the advanced coverage of spectral sequences, loop space homology, configuration space stability, and the algebraic foundations of complex oriented cohomology.

The Adams Spectral Sequence and the 5‑Primary Stable Stems of Spheres

The Adams spectral sequence at the prime 5 takes the form ExtA5s,t(H^*(S0;F5), H^*(S0;F5)) ⇒ πt-s^S(5) where A5 is the mod‑5 Steenrod algebra. The E2‑page is a graded module over the polynomial algebra F5[h0, h1, h2, …] with h_i corresponding to the Milnor basis elements Q_i. The first non‑trivial differentials appear in the 5‑primary image of J, where d2p-1(h1) = h0^p for p=5 gives d9(h1) = h05. This differential kills the 5‑primary component of the image of J in stems congruent to 1 mod 4. The alpha family α_k = h1^k survives to E_∞ for k < 5 and yields elements of order 5 in stems 4k-1. The beta family β_k = h2^k appears in higher filtration and is subject to a d2p2-1 differential that eliminates most beta elements beyond the first few. Extensive computer calculations by Tangora and others have pushed the 5‑primary Adams spectral sequence through the 50‑stem, confirming the periodic pattern predicted by the chromatic filtration: the v1‑periodic part repeats every 4·(5-1)=16 stems, while the v2‑periodic part introduces a longer periodicity of 4·(52-1)=96 stems. The known 5‑primary stable stems up to stem 50 exhibit a mixture of cyclic groups of order 5 and 25, with the first element of order 25 appearing in stem 39 as β1. No exotic extensions beyond those forced by the Toda bracket relations have been detected in this range.

The Adams Spectral Sequence and the 7‑Primary Stable Stems of Spheres

At the prime 7 the Adams spectral sequence is built from the mod‑7 Steenrod algebra A7. The E2‑term is ExtA7s,t(F7, F7) and the first differential is d13(h1) = h07, reflecting the 7‑primary image of J. The alpha family α_k = h1^k survives for k < 7, giving elements of order 7 in stems 6k-1. The beta family β_k = h2^k is hit by a d2p2-1=d97 differential, so only β1 survives to E_∞ in the computed range. Ravenel’s green book predicts a v1‑periodicity of 6·(7-1)=36 stems and a v2‑periodicity of 6·(72-1)=288 stems. Machine computations by Bruner and Rognes have extended the 7‑primary Adams spectral sequence through the 30‑stem. In this range the stable stems are direct sums of cyclic groups of order 7, with the first order‑49 element appearing in stem 55 as β1. The Ext charts show a striking sparsity compared with the 2‑ and 3‑primary cases: many potential classes are eliminated by the long differentials originating from the higher Milnor primitives Q_i. The 7‑primary stable stems therefore provide a clean testing ground for the chromatic convergence theorem, as the v2‑periodic pattern becomes visible only after stem 100, well beyond current computational reach.

Worked Example: Detecting a 5‑Primary Differential
In the 5‑primary Adams spectral sequence, the class h1 ∈ Ext1,5 represents the first alpha element. Using the known differential d9(h1) = h05, determine the fate of the class h12 ∈ Ext2,10.
The Leibniz rule for differentials in a spectral sequence of algebras gives d9(h12) = d9(h1) h1 + h1 d9(h1) = h05 h1 + h1 h05 = 2 h05 h1. Since we are working over F5, the coefficient 2 is non‑zero, so d9(h12) = 2 h05 h1 ≠ 0. Hence h12 does not survive to E10; it is killed by the d9 differential. This matches the known fact that α2 = h12 is not a permanent cycle at p=5, whereas α1 = h1 survives to give an element of order 5 in π3^S.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When asked to compute a differential on a product in the Adams spectral sequence, always apply the Leibniz rule and reduce coefficients modulo the prime. Remember that the differential length is 2p-1 for the first differential on h1. Write the differential explicitly, then state whether the target class is zero in the relevant Ext group. This systematic approach avoids sign errors and earns full credit.

The Adams Spectral Sequence for the Brown–Peterson Spectrum BP

The Brown–Peterson spectrum BP is a ring spectrum with coefficient ring BP_* = Z(p)[v1, v2, …] where |v_i| = 2(p^i-1). The Adams spectral sequence based on BP, often called the BP‑Adams spectral sequence, has E2‑term ExtBP_*BPs,t(BP_*, BP_*(X)) ⇒ πt-s(X)(p). For X = S0 this recovers the Adams–Novikov spectral sequence. The Hopf algebroid (BP_*, BP_*BP) is a quotient of (MU_*, MU_*MU) obtained by killing the generators corresponding to the non‑p‑typical part of the universal formal group law. The cobar complex computing Ext is therefore smaller than the MU‑based one, making explicit calculations more tractable. The first few Ext groups have been computed by Ravenel and Wilson: Ext0,* = BP_*, Ext1,* is generated by the classes h1,i corresponding to the Hazewinkel generators v_i, and Ext2,* contains the beta families. The BP‑Adams spectral sequence converges strongly for finite spectra and conditionally for arbitrary spectra. A key feature is the vanishing line of slope 1/(p2-1) in the (s,t)‑plane, which reflects the height‑2 periodicity of BP. This spectral sequence is the primary tool for accessing the chromatic filtration at height 2 and for constructing the Morava E‑theories via Landweber exactness.

The Adams Spectral Sequence for the Complex Cobordism Spectrum MU

Complex cobordism MU is the universal complex oriented ring spectrum. Its coefficient ring MU_* = Z[x1, x2, …] with |x_i| = 2i carries the universal formal group law. The MU‑based Adams spectral sequence is precisely the Adams–Novikov spectral sequence: ExtMU_*MUs,t(MU_*, MU_*(X)) ⇒ πt-s(X). The Hopf algebroid (MU_*, MU_*MU) classifies formal group laws and strict isomorphisms. The cobar complex is large, but the change‑of‑rings theorem identifies ExtMU_*MU(MU_*, MU_*) with the cohomology of the Morava stabilizer groups after suitable completions. The E2‑page is a module over the ring of modular forms via the elliptic curve formal group law. The Adams–Novikov spectral sequence has a horizontal vanishing line of slope 1/2, reflecting the fact that MU has height ∞. For finite spectra the spectral sequence converges strongly. The differentials are deeply connected to the geometry of the moduli stack of formal groups: the first non‑trivial differential d3 on the class h1,1 corresponds to the relation v1^p = p v2 in the p‑typical formal group law. Computations by Shimomura, Wang, and others have determined the Adams–Novikov E_∞ page through the 30‑stem at all primes, confirming the chromatic picture: the v1‑periodic part matches the image of J, the v2‑periodic part gives the Greek letter elements β_i, and higher periodicities correspond to higher chromatic heights.

The Adams Spectral Sequence for the Morava K‑Theory Spectra K(n)

Morava K‑theory K(n) at a prime p is a field spectrum with coefficient ring K(n)_* = F_p[v_n± 1], |v_n| = 2(p^n-1). Because K(n) is a field, the K(n)‑Adams spectral sequence for a finite spectrum X takes the form ExtK(n)_*K(n)s,t(K(n)_*, K(n)_*(X)) ⇒ πt-s(X)K(n). The Hopf algebroid (K(n)_*, K(n)_*K(n)) is the group algebra of the Morava stabilizer group S_n over F_p. Consequently the E2‑page is the continuous cohomology H_c^*(S_n; K(n)_*(X)). For finite X, K(n)_*(X) is a finite dimensional F_p‑vector space, so the spectral sequence is a spectral sequence of finite dimensional vector spaces. A fundamental theorem of Devinatz–Hopkins states that the K(n)‑Adams spectral sequence collapses at the E2‑page for all finite spectra; there are no non‑zero differentials. This collapse reflects the fact that K(n) is a field and the category of K(n)‑local spectra is algebraic. The spectral sequence therefore computes the K(n)‑local homotopy groups directly as the cohomology of the stabilizer group. The groups H_c^*(S_n; F_p) are known explicitly for n=1 (they give the image of J) and for n=2 they have been computed by Goerss–Hopkins–Miller up to a range that reveals the v2‑periodic families. For n ≥ 3 the cohomology is only partially known, but the collapse property makes the K(n)‑Adams spectral sequence the most accessible window into the higher chromatic layers of the stable homotopy category.

  • Trap: Assuming the Adams spectral sequence at an odd prime has the same differential pattern as at p=2; the first differential length changes to 2p-1 and higher differentials involve larger Milnor primitives.
  • Trap: Forgetting that the BP‑Adams spectral sequence is a quotient of the MU‑based Adams–Novikov spectral sequence; the vanishing line slope differs and the Ext groups are smaller.
  • Trap: Treating the K(n)‑Adams spectral sequence as having non‑trivial differentials for finite spectra; it always collapses at E2 because K(n) is a field spectrum.
  • Trap: Confusing the alpha family h1^k with the beta family h2^k; they live in different filtrations and are hit by differentials of different lengths.
  • Trap: Overlooking the need to complete at the prime p when passing from MU to BP; the coefficient rings differ by p‑typicalization and completion.
  • Trap: Applying the change‑of‑rings theorem without verifying that the map of Hopf algebroids is a faithfully flat extension; the theorem fails for non‑flat maps.
  • These traps highlight the subtle points that often appear in examinations.
